THE GOBLIN AND THE GRAVEDIGGER.

He went to tl•e graveyard on a Christmas Eve to dig a grave, as ‘-feeling very low he thought it might raise his spirits." He scowled on tlie signs of festivity about him as he went, and, meeting & boy singing for joy, rapped him over the head with a lantern. And on Christmas Eve, with the world full of joviality and good cheer, Gabriel Grub plied his spade on a new grave, and sang with pleasure over his erniet,er task. It wee in the midst of this pleasant task that the goblin found him. It was this quaint, eerie being who asked: "What man wanders amongst graves and churchyards on such a night as this?" and a wild chorus of voices which seemed to fill the graveyard screamed "Gab- riel Grub! Gabriel Grub!" "Who drinks hollanda alone and in a churchyard on such a night as this?" said the goblin. "Gabriel ,Grub! Gabriel Grub!" exclaimed the wild voice* again. "And who then is our fair and lawful prize?" he asked; and again came the wild chorus, "Gabriel Grub! Gab- riel Grub!" • So the goblins claimed the gravedigger and whisked him off to Goblinland. There they skewed him men and women destined to stir his heart and fire his brain with a mellow humanity; and, led by the head goblin, the world of sprites emphasised each picture by kicking the unhappy sexton. Finally, "above all, he saw that men like himself, who smarted at the mirth and cheerfulness of others were

....... the foule,st weeds on the fair surface of the earth"; and, having been kicked without mercy, was returned from Goblinland to the world to lead a better life.

Such was the first characteristic ghost story of Dickens. Distinctly and clearly in it are foreshadowed all the characteristics of '

THE GREATEST OF ALL GHOST STORIES, A Christmas Carol." The whole end and aim of the greater story is the same as in the slighter story of Gabriel Grub. The idea underlying the chastening of the sexton is .the same which runs through the purging of Scrooge's evil heart. The earlier story was simply a careless fabric, the later story a classic. The construction is the same. The same dour hero at war with the human fes- tivities of the world; the same timely ghost to grip him and carry the stony heart to beat near the warm hearts of homely, living people; the same sharp contrast between sel- fishness and unselfishness; the same rounding of the moral that he who lives in charity takes his pay in love and esteem, are ap- parent in both of these Christmas tales. And after the soured man awakens, to find the phantoms but spectres of an . ugly dream, there is the same result. On Gabriel Grub's awakening, we are casually told "he was an altered man, and he could not bear the thought of returning to A place where his re- pentance would be scoffed at and his reform- ation disbelieved. He hesitated for a few moments, and then turned away to wander where he might, and seeik his bread else- , where." The story, so far as the moral it teaches, is but carelessly applied. Gabriel Grub runs away to seek a better outlook. In the larger story,, "A Christmas Carol," the characters, living and ghostly, move on a larger platform; and, when the good is wrought in Scrooge he plays the man, and fights out his battle against self by doing un- selfish things amongst the people he has wronged. The germ of "A Christmas Carol,'• first called to life in the story of Gabriel Grub, lies like a seed through winter; waiting for a fuller power to call it to more perfect bloom.

Old Chriatrras Rhymes.—Those who grieve over the decay of the old-fashioned Christmas with its wassail bowls and family parties, many perhaps find some comfort in the fact that there always were people who lamented the departure of the" brave days of old." Thus we find a rhymester of 17:36 saying that but for the alrnanack, " Christmas scarcely should we know," and in bitterest doggerel declaring " How happy were those days so old. When feasting did all twelve days old." According to an old Scottish rhyme, Christmas was at one time a popular festival in Scotland for it lays :

" Yule's come and Yule's game. And we hao feasted weel ; Sae Joch maim to his flail again, And Jenny to her wheel." Christmas Fensting.—The medical journals usually sound a rote of warning at this season about the harm- fulness of too much good cheer. They have nothing very terrible to say about plum pudding, which, by pro- longed boiling, is rendered comparatively easy of digestion, and according to one of these authorities you can stand any number of indigestible dishes provided you feast cheerfully. If you are alone "gloomy, sullen, stupid "as Robert Burns wrote, you had better eat sparingly, but it is mainly a question of environment, and if you are happy and jolly, a surfeit of good and normally indigestible things will do no harm. Laughter says the doctor, is a cure for indigestion, and nervous diseases, but of course it does not come easy to the confirmed nervous dyspeptic. " Don't be miserable, don't worry,don't be poor,don't he ill, don't die " is all good adaice if one can act up to it, but as a rule the stfferer finds it very difficult to do this, at any rate, as far as could be wished. Still, to most people there comes a mood of Christmas joviality, and it is some- thing to know, that if you feast cheerfully, you may feast with impunity. More Fatty Food.—At one time, the suet pudding was regarded as suitable food only for strong men who lived strenuous lives in the open air, but we have changed all that, and the doctors now tell us that the well-made well-cooked suet pudding, is a perfect food for all people. Especially they say is this so in winter when we often fail to include a sufficiency of fat in the diet. Cold feet, bands, fingers, ears, end chilblains, could often—so the men of medicine tell us now—be avoided under a generous diet of fatty food. A Meal Before Retiring.—Many of us can remember the days when we were taught that anything taken after a seven to eight o'clock rreal in the evening was little better than poison. One of the chief sins against one's stomach was giving it work to do just before nutting it to bed. Put now, with a conscience perfectly at rest, we may, if we choose, sip a cup of hot soup or milk. and nibble a small crust of bread, before retiring. Undoubtedly the procedure described is an excellent one, for the thin or delicate, or for those who suffer from sleeplessness, while the woman past her youth with hollow cheeks and eyes, will speedily find the cup of bob milk, taken the last thing most beneficial. About Truffles.—With mach feasting in progress, we frequently see the truffle mentioned, and many unaccustomed to " high living " will no doubt wonder yet again, what this particular dainty may be. As a matter of fact the truffle is nothing but a mushroom, but it has one great pectitiarity, it grows beneath the soil, and often at considerable depth. It is found chiefly under oak trees, but also beneath birches, hazels, and beeches. The precious and costly fungus is black in appearance, its flesh, according to the varieties, is grey, yellow, or brown, or even black. Its taste—" gourmets" say—is most exquisite, but It must be confessed that the majority of people "see nothing in it." Presents for menkind.—Knitted silk ties and mufflers are, when well done, acceptable presents to menkind generally, and for weeks past many fair fingers have been wrestling with halls of silk, and knitting needles. The King prefers a knitted silk scarf to all other varieties of neck wraps, a state of affairs that has gone far to make it exceedingly popular. Needless to say, the work should be perfectly even and smooth, and to ensure this, the tips of the firgors should receive care- ful attention always, before taking it up. Further, the worker should make a point of sitting in an excellent light, as dropped stitches frequently spell ruin, however Well "picked oz." A Novelty.— the new evening hoods will be especi- ally welcomed by suburban women, who have a journey by train or omnibus to undertake before arriving at the theatre or restaurant. These hoods appear to the casual observer perfectly soft, and almost shapeless, but in reality they are mounted upon a light wire frame, which keeps the hair from being crushed or disarranged. They can, of course, be carried out as expensively or inexpensively as the wearer may desire, but the best match the cloaks, under which they can so easily be slipped when entering a restaurant or theatre. Youthful Debutantes.—Balls and dances will fill the early days of the New Year in many of the great country houses, where large family parties have assembled for the festive season. Lady Salisbury will give a ball at Hatfield House on .Tanuary 13th, which will witness the " coming out " of her eldest daughter. Lady Beatrice Cecil, and also of several other young girls included among the guests. It is usual now for quite young girls to make an informal entry into Society at country house parties and dances before making their maiden bow at Court. Lady Beatrice Cecil is a tall, aoed-looking girl of 17, two years older than her brother, Lord Cranborne, and another important young lady who will make her formal appearance in Society at one of the tato Courts next year is Lady Victoria Stanley, the eldest daughter of Lord and Lady Derby. Like her mother, Lady Victoria is a particular favourite of Royalty, and she has been a frequent guest at the queen's parties for yonng people at Buckingham Palace. She will be seventeen next June, only a few months younger than Mies Mary Colebrooke, daughter of Lord and Lady Colebrooke, vho was presented at Court this year, although she bas only recently celebrated her , aeventeentb birthday. Whist Drives.—All sorts of Fames are invented to while away winter evenings but whilst new inventions come and go, cards go on for ever, as the most popular form of indoor amusement. Hitherto, whist drives, and bridge compet lone, have been confined almost exclusively to private parties, but whist drives are now being organised on a public basis in London. large halls are taken, substantial prizes are offered, and the players •are admitted by ticket for which a fee is charged. Some doubt arises as to whether such entertainments can be legal, but they appear to be becoming popular, in spite of the risk of meeting at such gatherings undesirable people, or those who make use of unfair methods. One has an assurance at private parties against any unplensantna!-s of tiais kind, Lot, as all public dances are not to be condemned because some are of a free and easy kind, so perhaps there may be oportunities for the public whist drive as a social form of amusement. A DEATHLESS TALE.

"A Christmas Carol" was published in 1843, yet to-clay the story is as fresh as ever. It carries with it the same weight and the same warmth. In its short compass it con- tains all that can be said of the English Christmas. Its ghosts are good ghosts. They are none of your headless spectres with manacled fists. They do not gibber or scream, walk dismal corridors, or wail in lonely high- ways. In fact, saving Marley's ghost, they are not orthodox' at all; and the only ortho- dox point about Marley's grizzly shadow is that he was Chained. Even the chain is merely symbolical—he dragged behind him a burden of cash-boxes, keys, padlocks, ledgers, deeds, rind heavy purses, the burdens which chained the soul of Scrooge in life. And even when Dickens brings in the creepy spectre, he cannot resist the burlesque touch which re- duces it at once to the realms of the fantastic. His body was so transparent that Scrooge, observing him, and looking through his waist- coat, "could see the two -buttons on his coat behind. Scrooge had often heard it said that Marley had no bowels, but he had never be- lieved it until now." And with that quota- tion, Marley's ghost at once becomes less a thing of terror and more a human, com- panionable ghost, with a pleasant mission in view. He was the forerunner of three more ghosts, and serves more as an amiable intro- duction than a thing of terror, and one knows instinctively that only good can come out of his visit.

But the three ghosts that follow have a mellow, dignified character. There was the ghost of Christmas Past, so gentle; the ghost .of Christmas Present, so jovial; and the ghost of Christmas Yet to Come, silent and dignified, emphasising in stern terms the lesson of his preceding brothers of the years. They are

===== 0002601_19081224_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT GHOSTS.

None of your childish, illogical bogies, haunting mysteriously, wailing aimlessly, and terrorising out of sheer wantonness. They have in hand a glorifked typo of Gabriel Grub, a man of substance in the city, schose cash-box, safe, and bank book have become his idols, excluding any human note.! And they have this man to shew him the real world of joy and sorrow, pleasure and pain,' and the triumphant reign of the Unselfish over the Selfish man.

How many thousands of people have Ntoured with Scrooge through his wanderings with the I ghosts? - How many have forgotten their ghostliness in learning the lesson of their de- tached goodness? HOw many have learnt, with Scrooge, the faculty of kindliness, and the golden secret which is the reward of kindliness—that generous glow withiff- one's purified self? The pictures the ghost showed in the weird panorama to wring the heart of Scrooge and to sh-ew him the joys he had missed; the pictures grouped to show him the. evil he had (lone; the pictures conjured up to shew him the inevitable end—the lonely corpse and the neglected grave—have stirred the same generous emotions which moved Dickens to the writing of them. And who, following the Crachite through their humble pleasures, suffering their humble agonies, and sharing in their restoration to the light and life of the world, have not said in glad relief, "God bless us every one !"? Ghosts—the ghosts of everything that is good and holy, the ghosts of things of good repute, the ghosts of human sympathy and understanding, the ghosts of pity and love and charity—they are the spirits which stand in the shadow watching our poor humanity, and eyml,athißing-every.tire4 when its triumph is ^. kindly motive. They are not ghosts which Dickens built up in "A Christmas Carol," nor eerie shadows destined to make purpose- less horrors. They are the spirits of the better things of life; the echoes which the good man keeps sounding .in his heart while the evil man chokes them into silence; the pictures of a bravo man's brain, clothed in mystic symbols, which move through' • the years and link the name of Dickens with-the natural kindliness of men which wells eternally, happily for us all, within tho htunan heart.

THE FISH-POND. Another simple and highly attractive way of opening the entertainment is to provide a fish-pond for the little ones. Take an ordinary clothes-basket and drape it, side and out, with crumpled green paper. This is the pond. In it place a number of little in- expensive toys, wrapped separately in green paper and tied lightly round with string. A fishing-rod can be made out of a stick to which is fastened a piece of string with a bent pin at the end. The children gather round the pond in turn, and trail the line among the frshe.s until one becomes en- tangled in the hook. The achievement affords as much delight as does the reception of the little present, and thus the pleasure, given is twofold.

chairs, placed back to back, and a table- - cloth, will make a little cell in which sits the player chosen for the hermit. The leader of the game whispers to the hermit some ordi- nary descriptive word, such as "surly,"" "fat," " amiable." All the • players then form a procession and walk past the hermit's ce11,,. peeping in, and passing remarks upon him.. For instance, "Doesn't he look solemn!" "Oh, isn't he thin!" The remarks wilt cause great fun; but the object of each player is to avoid using the word 'agreed upon by the hermit and the loader. Directly the- word is used, by one in the procession, the hermit leaps from his cell and chases him., round and round the -standing line of players until he catches him. The one caught then becomes the hermit, and the game starta- • again. If a girl is caught the players use the word "she" in their remarks. THE SAILING SHIP. Among fireside games, suitable for older - players, "The Sailing Ship" is an excellent one. All the players sit in line, except the leader, •who faces them. The leader then put e the following questions to each in turn,. allowing only five seconds for the answer. Q.'(l) Mv ship has sailed to a place be-- ginning with A? •A. 'the reply must be some place begin- Ding with A, as Amsterdam. Q. (2) The captain's name begins with A? I A. Any name beginning with -A, as Adam.. I Q. (3) The ship's cargo begins with A? A. The first which occurs to the player, as Ashes.. - Q. (4) What shall my ship bring back be. ginning with A? A. Anything Which occurs, as Acid drops. When the four questions in A: have been answered, the leader starts the same four' with D; and so on. The answers, given rapidly, will evoke much laughter. He who fails to give ari answer in the five seconds is . passed over, forfeiting his place to the player who answers correctly. The player who is top at the end of the game receives a prize.. PICTURE TITLES. • A really fascinating amusement, for chil- dren or grown-ups, \is the guile of "Picture Titles." Each player is provided with a sheet of paper and a pencil. In the space of five minutes he meet make a rough sketch to, illustrate the title of some well-known story.. For,example, a man lighting a lamp will re- present ," The Lamplighter." A 'large - man,- and several tiny woman will represent "Little Women." A shop front, in Which are suspended a few odd articles, will repre7 sent "The Old Curiosity -ShoP.", and_ 60, , on. The greater The ingenuity shown in the • choice of titles the better the fun. When the drawings are finished each player has to - gams the titles represented, writing down the name of the sketcher and the title opposite. The player who has written the most correct answers in a given time reeciv,ea a 'prize, • geiK.l ally a book of some Lind.

'TALTON COURT LEET.

The triennial meeting of Halton Court Leet, was held in the old courthouse as Halton Castle Hotel, near Runcorn, on Saturday afternoon. The court has jurisdiction over a large area of Cheshire, and includes townships on the borders of Altrincham, Knutsford, Northwich, and Crewe, and there was a good attend- ance, including two "freeman " who bad travelled from Hale and Holmes Chapel. His Majesty, as Duke of Lancaster and Baron of Halton is Lord Paramount of the manor and fee, and the high bailiff, Mr. Joseph Walter, made iiroclamation ancient and quaint, calling upon "all manner of persons who owe suit and service and view of frank pledge to draw near, give your attendance, and answer to your names." Mr. Vere- Davies, of Warrington, the deputy high steward, con- ducted the proceedings. The jury wore sworn in, and Mr. Thomas Wright, of Halton, was unanimously elected foreman, a capacity in which he has acted for many years. Officers of the court were duly appointed, and Mr. Davies gave an address. After the business was concluded, the jury eat down to a good dinner, pro- vided by the high bailiff, and the chief toast was " The - Bing, as Lord of the Manor."

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THZ QUEEN_ AS Gni EXPERT._

The Queen considerably surprised the gen- tleman in chary of the jewellery section at the New Queensland Offices the other day, writes a London correspondent, by her wide knowledge of gems and their qualities. He has just had the honour of selling to the Queen a large tourmaline—a tourmaline being a stone scarcely to be distinguished from the emerald in its brilliancy and green colour. On this occasion and on d previous one he has talked at length with the Queen on the subject, and was surprised at the ex- tent of her Majesty's information. The Queen admired the orange sapphires from the Anti- ' podes very much, and added that she had al- ways believed blue to be the necessary colour of sapphires. Queensland, it seems, produces not only orange and blue, but pure white, green, and yellow sapphires. The Queensland gem specialist was not quite sure of the Queen's identity when she first inspected his gems. "Her Majesty," said he, was 60 simple and unaffected in manner that for fully five minutes I was in doubt whether she might not be a lady-in-attendance on the Queen." A SMART PARTY SUIT.

Moat of us, I fancy, are in the thick of children's parties, dances, and similar festivi- ties by now, and children's party attire has grown to be quite an important matter for the time being. Our sketch shews a particu- larly smart and natty little velveteen suit,

..._ --- .•

specially designed with a view to such dis- sipations. It is carried out in velveteen of good quality (velvet may be used if liked, of course), which may be blank, white, or of some pretty colour, according to individual taste. The little coat is quite plain, fastens at the neck, but opens a little lower down, and shews a smart and most manly waistcoat of white piqué or silk. From the neck turns back a wide collar of the finest and most gossamer lawn, edged all round with lace, which is most effective when it is of rather a heavy type, such as Irish crochet or imitation Venetian point. The little trousers are wide at the top, but fit closely about the knees. This suit looks particularly well earritml„ out in grey velveteen.

A PRETTY INDOOR FROCK.

Pictured in our illustration is a particularly pretty and becoming frock for smart after- noon wear. It is realised in fine cloth of medium weight, and of a charming shade of mole—rather a light tone, with more brown than grey in its composition. The cloth part of the bodice is cut away to the merest no- thing, leaving simply a strap over each shoul- der and just a small amount of cloth under the arms and in front. Inside this cloth bodice comes a gathered under-bodice of the softest satin charmeuse in exactly the sarre shade. Juside _ azain_earnes _a •diritrty itttte chemisette of Irish crochet, white, of course, finished with a thick ruffling of net at the

top. The sleeves are almost tight-fitting, and are of full leng,th. A sash belt of mole satin defines the waist, and is drawn into a buckle in front, from which hang two very long ,tas- el led ends of the satin. The skirt is quite plain, rather high-waisted, very long all round, and slightly trained at the back.

THE REIGN OF THE COESGTIERE.

There never was a time when fashions better befriended the corsetihT, declares tire Queen. She is consulted. before dressmaker or tailor, and yet, with all her skill, she can- not make a fat woman thin, though it is per- fectly wonderful what she can do towards making her appear so. The most skilled can- not make her look like a sylph. Hips must disappear and waists become flat. Of course, when the hips are strapped in by the long coutille bands, the waist must grow larger; the too solid flesh refuses to melt. The high- waisted and the princess grce.vns tend to half conceal the waist, and if the hips are flat the wearer can present a good appearance. Some thin women assist the staymaker by having the skirt attached to some wide belt which fits the figure, leaving the. skirt to hang in straight lines from the top, and the short- -waisted gown looks better when attached to its own girdle two or three inches above the normal waist. We have done with linings fcr skirts, which help in the crucial point o; the skirts setting well.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

AN EGO AND SPINACH DAINTY.—Wash a handful of fresh spinach leaves and poach them for a few minutes in butter. In the meantime prepare a teacupful of white sauce, made with a little butter and as much flour as the butter will absorb, milk, salt, pepper, a few drops of lemon juice, and, if possible, a dessert-spoonful of cream. Grate two ounces each of Gruyere and Parmesan, and stir half into the sauce. Butter a flat fire-proof French dish, lay the spinach leaves at the bottom, cover with the rest of the cheese, break three eggs carefully on the top, and pour sufficient sauce over them to cover them. Sprinkle with fine breaclerumbs, lay small pieces of butter over the top, and cook in a moderate oven.

BY GEORGE EDGAR.

RAILWAY RING.

An excellent game for, the tiny tots is the "Railway Ring." Thread a small curtain ring on to a piece a string, and tie the ends. Boys and girls form a ring, holding the string in front of them. One player stands in the centre. Those in the ring, raise their hands, with a swinging side-to-side motion, ,and sing, to any well-known tune that fits: • ‘Johnny to, town has never been, .The. big wide world has. never .seen; He longs to see the great machine . That travels along the railway." *e circle of string is the railway. The ring is the machine! which Johnny, in the centre, tries to catch sight of as it is passed stealthily from hand to band. When he can call out the name of the child whose hand is covering it, he takes the place of the detected one, who becomes Johnny, and the game starts again.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
FAIRY BELLS.

A good, lively game for boys and girls is called "Fairy Bells." For this, a little tinkling bell is required. A boy stands in the centre, and all nisi other players form a ring round him. They stand shoulder to shoulder, with their hands behind them, singing: "Fairy bells are ringing, Fairy voices singing; Brother mortal, can you tell . - Where is now the fairy bell?

As they sing, they pass the bell about be- hind them, shaking it c.s it goes. When the centre-boy can detect the possessor of the bell he calls out the name, and the, two change places. Should the child in the centre now be a girl, the word " sister " is substi- tuted for brother" in the verse.

THE MOUSE HUNT. For the very little ones ylent .of 'boisterous fun - is provided by the Hunt.'- All the players stand in a ring, with the exception of two, one of whom is Pussy, the other Mouse. The hunt starts with the Mouse inside the ring and Pricsy outside. Pussy has to catch Mouse; but while those in the ring make way for Mouse es she darts in and out, under, their clasped bends, they re- main still when Pussy comes, refusing to give her any assistance. When Mouse is caught the pair between whom, she last passed be- come Pussy and Mouse.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
GRANDMOTHER'S BAG.

An exceedingly merry game for players of all ages is the • one called "My• Grand- mother's Bag." The leader sits facing the line of players, and addresses the opening re- mark to the first of the line, who must re- peat the remark and add on to it the name of some article. For example: Leader be- gins: "I packed my grandmother's bag with a hair-brush. First player repeats: "I packed .my grkndmother's bag with • a hair -brush and a toast-rack" (or any article). Second player immediately takes it up: "I packed my grandmother's bag with hair-brush, a tcast-rack, and a feather." This is repeated by the next piny r. who, in turn, adds on another article, till list srow- ing longer and funnier with each repetition. The player who fails to repeat the list of articles correctly pays a forfeit.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROSE QUEEN.

A graceful ring game, .of the sentimental kind, is "The Wee Queen."- The players form a ring. In the -centre stands a boy, holding a wreath of paper rc!7es. The ring mov'es round, singing (to any well-known tune which fits):

" Who shall he your Rose Queen? Who shall wear the crown? s Every girl is longing; Fling your roses down." They istand, and the boy advances to the chosen girl, bending the knee, and flinging the wreath at her f.ect. She picks it up, and the pair go into the centre, while the ring moves round, singing : "Eva (or any other) is our Rose Queen, Queen of hearts to-day. Crown her with your reses, Kiss her tears away." The girl is crowned and kissed, and th% pair join the ring. The next boy then goes to the centre, and the game starts again. -

===== 0002601_19081224_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
BRUSHING DOWN.

A game provoking shrieks of laughter—a favourite among the young folks of- Germany —is. called " Brushing Down." The players stand .clese togetlper in a • ring, with their hands behind them, so as to conceal an ordi- nary clothes 'brush, which is passed from one to. another. In the centre stands the one who is supposed to require a good brush down. It is his task to obtain possession of the brush. The fun grows louder and merrier as he turns from one to another, only to receive a brisk brushing on the back from another direction. When he succeeds in securing the brush, he takes the place of the possessor, who in turn goes into the centre.

FOX AND GEESE.- , Another game full of fun and excitement h called "Fox and Geese." In the centre of a ring of children .stand one called the fox. Facing the fox is another player, called the goose, with six players as gcslings standina behind her in line. 'nue in the ring 'wit& round

THAT WINTER COUGH! -- Cold Weather Scourge Banished by Peps.

- Thousands of people dread the arrival of the cold weather because they know it means for them a return of that awful winter cough. NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL Annual Meetings at Swansea.

The fourteenth Annual Council of the Evangelical Free Churches will be held in Swansea on March Bth, •oth, 10th and 11th, 1909. The arrangements are now being pressed forward, and the variety of the subjects to be delivered and the importance of the proceedings will appeal to Free Churchmen all over the Kingdom. The Sessions and Demonstrations will be held in the Albert Hall, and the Churches in Swansea and district 'will be also used for public meetings. Rev. Evan Jones, of Carnarvon, will preside. Sermons will be preached by Hey. David Brook, M.A., D.C.L., Rev. J. H. Jowett, ILA., Rev. H. Elvit Lewis. M.A., Rev. G. Campbell Morgan, M.A., D.D. Rev. Thomas Phillips, 13. A., and Rev. T. Rhondda, Williams. # Amongst the important subjects that will be brought forward for consideration are the following :—" Theo- logical Unrest and Organised Christianity " by Rev. W. B. Selbie, M.A., "The Historical Contribution of the Church to Social Reform " by Professor A. E. Garvie' M.A., D.D.," The Relative Duty of the Church and of the State to Poverty" by Rev. J. Scott Lidgett, , M.A., President of the Wesleyan Conference, The Brotherhood Movement and the Churches" by Rev. Harry Bisseker, "The Alarming Developments of Modern Romanism" by Rev. Joseph Hocking, " Report of the Liverpool Commission" by Mr. Arthur Black, " Con stitution Resolution" by Mr. Robert Whyte and Rev. -George Hooper, "Rev. F. B. Meyer, 8.A.0n his Mission Tour in South Africa," " The Influence of Present Day Life on Personal Religion," "The Place of Evangel in the Ministry of To-day." The Education Question, the Licensing Question and the House of Lords will probably receive prominent attention. The Official Report of the year's work will be presented by the Secretary, Req. Thomas Law. The adoption of the Report will be moved by the Rev. John McNeill. The Finance Report and Balance Sheet will be pre- sented by the Chairman of the Finance Cammittee, Sir J. G. Compton-Rickett, M.P., D.L. At the Devotional Session the Rev. Charles Brown (President of the Baptist Unson) and Rev. R. F. Horton, M.A., D.D., will deliver addresses. The social side of these gatherings will be emphasized on Monday evening, March Bth, when a Reception will be given to the delegates by the Mayor of Swansea. A Special Conference for Free Church Council Secre- taries and a Conference of Women Workers will also be held. The evening Public Meetings to be held in Swansea include the following :—Young People's Demonstra- tion, English Free Church Demonstration, Welsh Free Church Demonstration, Missionary Meeting, Women's Meeting and Temperance Meeting. The following among others are expected to take part .in these gatherings :—Sir S. T. Evans, N.C., M.P., Rev. Newton J. Marshall, M.A., Ph.D., Sir Alfred Thomas, M.P., Rev. Thomas Hughes, Mr. W. Macalpine J.P., Rev. R. Wardlaw Thomson, n.D., Rev. A. T. GAtery, Dr. John Clifford, Mrs. Lloyd George, Mrs. W. S. Caine, Mrs. Herbert Lewis, Mrs. George Cadbury, Mrs. H. 'Price Hughes, Mrs. Princ. Edwards, and Mrs. Charlotte Benham. It is being arranged for Public Meetings to be held on the closing Thursday night in all the towns near Swansea. The speakers who will take part in the Public Demonstrations and Sessions at Swansea, and the District Meetings, will include the leaders of all • the denominations in addition to those already announced. The well-known hospitality of Swansea will be taxed to the utmost to provide for the large number of repro- ' sentatives expected. As on past occasions, it is expected that fare and a quarter rates will be afforded by the Railway Companies. Membership Tickets, etc. will be issued by Rev. Thomas Law at the National Council Headquarters, Memorial Hall, London, E.C. Shop Theft. At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, Albert -Edward Allen, residing at 20, Lythgoes•lane, was charged with stealing a ladies' coat, valued at 4s. 6d. from the shop of Messrs. Park and Bowdon, Butter-' market-street, at about 8.30 the previous night.—The Chief Constable stated that about five past ten the previous night P.C. Burrows was on duty in Butter- market-street whsn he saw the prisoner standing opposite the Waggon and horses Inn with the jacket over his arm. The constable asked him where he not it from, and prisoner said be had bought it. The constable took prisoner to Messrs. Park and Bowden's shop, and they thereupon recognised the coat as one which had been stolen earlier in the evening.—John Bowden was called and said he was partner in the 'shop at 30, Buttermarket-street. At 8.30 the previous - night the coat was seen hanging outside the shop on a -coat hanger. Ile was quite sure the coat had not been sold to the prisoner. —ln answer to the charge prisoner said he was going home to his lodgings,when he was stopped by a man who offered to sell him the coat. He gave him eightpenca for it. —Prisoner, who had been 6 tints previously before the courts, was sent to gaol for one mouth with hard labour.

SYSTEM.

At a meeting of the School Management Coma ittee a letter was read from the Board of Education in refer- ence to the Committee's proposal to amalgamate the two Girls' departments et the Fairfield school, and the Director was requested to draft a reply thereto for submission to the Committee at their next meeting. It was resolved (a) That, in view of the altered con- ditions governing admission to Training Colleges and of the difficulty which is being experienced by pupil teachers at the end of their engagements in gaining admission to colleges, the Committee regret that they will have to take into consideration the desirability of discontinuing the present pupil teacher system ; and (to) that the director be requested to report to the Committee at their next meeting upon the whole question. A communication from the Earl of \Loath (which had been forwarded by his Worship the Nltyor)—ia refer- enoo to the celebration of " Crapiro Day," 1903—was submitted, and it was decided to inform the Ntlyor that the Committee will be prepared to make similar arrange- ments for the celebration of the occasion in the schools to those made in previous years, It was noted that an instalment of grant for the school year 1037-3, amounting to £175, had been paid by the Bard of I!: liostion to the Governors of the Wirrington Gram net School. Councillor LORD referred to the minute with refer- ence to the grant being received by the governors of the Grammar School from the Board of Education. He would like to know whether the Board of Elucation supplied the Committee with copies of the reports they sent to the governors of the school and if not, did the Committee require the governors to furnish them with copies ? He meant the reports on the efficiency of the scnool, . .

The C11,111211,1N : This is meraly an interim grant. It is not a final grant. The DIRECTOR said the Committee had not had any reports from the Board of Education on the Grammar School but he believed the governors of the Grammar School had had copies of letters to the Committee on the con duct of the Secondary School. He presumed in the case of a report the Committee would receive a copy from them. Mr. BURGESS, as a repressntative of the Grammar School, said the origin il report made by his NI tjesty's Inspector was sent to the Committee fonr years ago and there hid been no inspection of thu school by the Board of Education since then. . . .

Councillor PEMBERTON : How is the grant obtained? Is it not made on the efficiency of the school ? If so, how is the efficiency arrived at ? The DIRECTOR : The inspections are made every three years. Councillor PEMBERTCII: Is it true the Education Committee received a copy of the report lr. Bargees referred to ? The Drnacrort said the committee received report made on the inspection of the school with a view to its coming under the Board of Education. Mr. BURGESS : That's the only one there has been. .

Councillor PEMBERTON : 'Mutt report on the ethel- cube of the school do they make the grants upon ? The CITAIRMIN : I believe the inspection is expected every day and the report will come before us. The DIRECTOR explained that after the first inspec- tion the grants were to be paid for three years and re-assessed at the end of that period. Mr. ROBERTS said that as the result of the inspection, the Board of Education made certain conditions which had to be complied with as a condition of receiving a grant. Those conditions involved certain structural alterations and an aldition to the curriculum and staff of the school and subject to the governors complying with them the Board of Education recognized the school as a secondary school entitled to a grant which would be payable until a further inspection had been made as to the efficiency of the school generally, having regard to the changes. If the school was found to be inefficient the grant would in all probability be docked or stopped. Councillor LORD : Do I understand these grants are made for throe years and are not dependent at all upon he efficiency of the school ? Are they different from he elementary schools and the technical schools ? . . . .

The Drßucrioa said the Socondary school, the School of Art, and the Grammar school were all on the same lines. An inspection was made and for three years the grant was allowed to go on. Mr. Upsos said gr. Burgess assured him that the school had been visited by inspectors but no report h id been made on the work. They had satisfied themselves that the work was efficient.

Canon STEVENSON referred to the decision of the Committee to consider the desirability of discontinuing the present pupil teacher system and adopting the bursar and student teacher system, oMterving that it was a matter which affected the future of a good many of their young people. The School Management Com- mittee had unanimously decided that the time had now come when they must make a change from the old pupil teacher system to the new system of bursars and student teachers. That was to say that a bay after four years at a Secondary School,on passing an examina- tion became what was called a bursar, which meant that he had another year in the Secondary school. After that he might become a student teacher and employ himself mainly in the work of one of the schools in the town, to learn the business of teaching, or he might enter a training college at once. The former of these methods was usually adopted. The reason why the Committee's hand was forced to make the change was that the bursars at the conclusion of their bursary year passed the preliminary certificate examination which qualified them to enter a training college. They then applied to some college and, if satis- factory, they were promised admission, whereas the pupil teachers had to work on for another year before they passed the preliminary certificate examination, and when they had passed it was quite possible they might find all the colleges already full, and through no fault of their own would be unable to airt admission. There- fore though to many of the Committee the old pupil teacher system seemed the better of the two, yet they felt it was impossible for them to fight against the general htrearn of tendency of opinion in the country, and as almost every education authority had adopted the new system, they must not I lace their young people at a great disadvantage, and they therefore felt they were bound to adopt the new idea. The minutes were approved. THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

THE SCHOOL ACCOMMODATION QUESTIOg.

At a meeting of the School Attendance Committeem a matter of urgency, it was reported that the resolution passed by this Committee at their last meeting in connection with the arrangements for the feeding of school children, had been referred back to the Education Committee by the Town Council, and, after some discussion, it was resolved, (a) That, in view of the fact that between 600 and 700 children attending public elementary schools in the Borough had been reported by the Head Teachers to be unable, by reason of lack of food, to take full advantage of the education provided for them, and that the work of providing the meals could not be commenced until February or !starch next, if the ordinary procedure were to be adopted, a deputation, consisting of the Chairman of this Com- mittee and the Chairman of the Education Committee be appointed to wait upon His Worship the Mayor and the Town Clerk, with a view, if possible, to obtaining sanction to proceed with the arrangements as speedily as possible; (b) that, in the event of this sanction being received, authority be given for the immediate purchase of the apparatus which has already been approved by this Committee ; nod that the Sub-Com- mittee be empowered to complete the arrangements so that the meals may be supplied as soon as possible; and (c) that the committee's previous resolution be re-affirmed,

On the recommendation of the Sub-Committee, it was further resolved that the following articles be purchased, viz. a soup boiler at a cost of £l7 12s. 6d. and a porridge boiler at a cost of £25 ; (b) 4 pate boxes at a cost of £3 11s. ; (c) 4 bread boxes a': a cost of £2 us. ; (d) 1 broad cutter at a cost of £l 54. 6d. ; (e) 1 potato peeler at a cost of £2 18s. 6d. ; and (f) 15 dozen mugs at Is. Bd. a dozen ; also that an advertisement be issued inviting applic Along for the post of male cook, at a weekly wage of 30s. He also saw Constance Nisbet' eyebrows , The CIIAIRNI AN wished all the members a very happy straighten when he sat down beside the . Her name he had noticed by his Christmas and a prosperous New Year and the wish I singer. Was heartily reciprocated by the Committee. 1 programme was Maud Lane. I "Don't you know anybody here?" ho

thc.) I of

asked. " Only the baritone," said the girl, with a little smile. ." He is a professional like my- self." "Don't these people know enough to give you anything to eat? I see coffee and things in the room yodder." "I clammy somebody will remember me by-and-bye. They have a good ninny amateurs to attend to, you see." "Then," said Beverley, right in there with me." . He held out his arm, and once more she wondered why she laid her hand upon it with so much confidence, while in another few minutes he set the best tnere was in tne buffet before her, and stayed until she ate it, talking energetically. Maud Lane, somewhat

"you are corning

to her astonishment, found herself talking gaily too, and it seemed to the man that her voice was as pretty as she was herself. At last he left her reluctantly, and met one of his British relatives lounging in a corridor, cigarette in hand. The latter grinned at him. "It's no great wonder Constance isn't quite pleased with you," he said. " Still, the girl—is—pretty. Where d'you pick her up?" "On the road," said Beverley, un- guardedly, end when his companion laughed turned upon him with an ominous flash in his eyes. "Well," he said, "what the deuce has that to do with you?" "Nothing," said the other man. "It may, however, have its effect upon Constance 's mother. You see, Miss Lane works for her living, and you admit you found her on the road. Things like that have a significance in this country. I presume you really mean tc marry Constance? Everybody expects you to." Beverley looked at him grimly. "Miss Nis- bet has, at least, never led ITIC to believe that she did, and until lately I've had to work quite hard for a living, too. I'm rather proud to remember it when I watch you folks fool your time away. As to the rest, if I can't get a girl who's tired and hungry something to eat without folks saying things about her, I'm going to make the first of them I catch at it The other man, who knew the Beverley temper, said nothing further; and Lance went back to his seat in the concert-room where Mrs. Nisbet, who seemed te have heard what he had done, favoured him with a little tactful lecture between the songs. She also intimated that Constance would not drive home with him that night. Beverley bore this patiently, and left a little before the rest, while it happened that Maud Lane, who had been kept by an encore, reached the station a minute too late for her train. That left her four miles to walk home, and she set about it with a little sigh, for she was very tired, and it was a hitter night. Still, she _had only just turned her back upon the town wfien a glare of light—felt --ttpv,. ber;arfd --1-15 F—Er mining motor stopped close by her side. Looking up she saw Beverley with his cap in his hand. "I guessed you would miss that train," he said. "Won't you get in?" The girl hesitated, but there was something in his voice that inspired confidence, and a few fakes of snow were driving before the stinging wind. Then suddenly her mind seemed made up for her, and she got in. The man packed the furs high about her with a pleasant laugh. "Now," he said, "you're snug, and we'll take the lower road. It's a better one, and I'm going to let this machine out." There was a rumbling beneath them, and the car sprang forward into the bitter night, hurling before it a long shaft of radiance through which the girl, who felt all the ex- hilaration of the mad rush, watched the gates and hedges flash. For a time she forgot the grim monotony of her toilsome life. She was young, after all, and the blood stirred in her while her cares fled away, and Beverley, lean- ing forward, with hard, uncovered hand upon the wheel, felt his pulse beat faster as he heard her laugh. They swept into the little straggling town almost too soon, and he touched her arm when he slowed the car.

seasons," she said. "You probably know rather more than I do about the advantages of consolidating the family property." There was a faint flush in Beverley's face, but he made a little grave gesture. "Thank you," he said, and left her, while the girl, who felt that the had wiped out a rather long score that night, laughed softly to herself.

It was a little later when Beverley found Constance Nisbet alone in the conservatory, though he had noticed Fulke come out a few moments earlier. .He drew a chair forward, and then leant against a flower stage looking down on her. "There are time when one lilts to be candid," he said. "You used to think a good deal of the man who has just been talking to you?" Constance Nisbet glanced at him with con- cern in her eyes. "I—" she said, and stopped abruptly. Beverley made a little gesture. "Well," he said, "I guess nobody would blame 'yen. He is a better-looking man than I am, and, if his face is anything to go by, a straight one. Now I'm going to be a little brutal, because it will save trouble for everybody. If it hadn't been for this confounded property you would have married that man?"

The girl looked at him with a little flash in her eyes. "Yes," she said, "it is, after all, better to tell you so." Beverley gravely took one of her hands. "Thank you. If things had been different you and I might have made out well enough together. There's a good deal that any man might be glad to have in his partner in you. Still, it wasn't to be that way—and you have got to be quite honest. Notthing would make you care in the least for me?" The girl gasped with relief, and then—for she had felt the tension—laughed almost harshly. "No," she said. "Are you sorry?" "We'll let that go," and Beverley made her a little inclination. "You could be happy with the other man, and it's your mother that is the difficulty. Well, she wouldn't want you to marry me if I hadn't that interest in the property?" The girl gazed at him with the crimson in her face, and Beverley laid his other hand on hers. "Now," he said, "you know it's so, and you have in several ways been kind to me. There can be no reason why you shouldn't take a Christmas girt from me. I'm going along to your father, and if you'll come back here in half-an-hour I shouldn't be astonished if you found it waiting for you." He went away, and five minutes later her father gazed at him in blank astonishment when he heard what he had to say. He, how- ever, saw that the man was perfectly serious, and sent for his wife; while, when at last Beverley went out after arranging to meet a lawyer during the next week, the pair sat silent for at -least a minute. Then Nisbet laughed curiously. "Nobody but a Beverley would have thought of the thing," he said. "You will excuse me saying that they were all a trifle crazy. But I must confess that I always liked young Fulke. There can, of course, be no objections to Constance marrying him now. And, after all, she had more right to the pro- perty, ethically, than Lance." Beverley, in the meantime, had flung a leather jacket over his evening clothes, and, springing into the car, sent it humming through the darkness into the little town, while, when he pulled it up before the secend- rate villa, he found Miss Lane alone. She rose when he came in, and he saw the faint colour in her thin face.

"It's 'most a pity we hadn't twenty miles to go," he said. "Still, we're here, and I'm coming for just a moment to be presented to your people. In a way, I think I owe it them." He stopped the car outside one of a row of secoad-rate villas, where the girl told him she and her sister had rooms. And he went in and found a woman with a delicate face, who seemed older than his companion, busy with a filmy mass of lace. His keen eyes also noticed the colour-box and pile of photo- graphs.on the side table. "Miss Lane, I presume?" he said. "I am Lance Beverley, a New Westminster, saw- mill engineer. It seemed the proper thing to tell you I had the honour of bringing your sister home."

"One would almost have fancied you had brought trouble enough on us," she said. "1 m afraid I have," said Beverley. " Still, how was I to know? Was it a very astonish- ing thing that I fell in love with .your sister as soon as I met her? Any way, you're going to give- me an opportunity of telling her so, and if she'll listen you're both of yo tr coming out to Canada with me."

The woman seemed a little astonished. But Beverley was seldom troubled by diffi- dence, and he stayed five minutes talking gaily, while, when he went out, Miss Lane glanced at her sister, who eat silent with a flush in her face.

"Was it very dreadful of me, Grace?" she asked, "Of course it was. But he was only kind, and. I was so tired, and felt rebellious to-night. Mrs. Cranston was horrid this afternoon, and kept me on purpose until I tnissed the train.' Then she broke off with a little bitter laugh, and a drop of moisture sparkled on her cheek. "Well," she said, "I must never do anything of that kind again. I can't afford it. It was my one mad adven- ture. After all, the fairy prince never turns up in real life:"

The resolution was easily made; but it proved another matter to keep it when she was overtaken by the car two or .three miles from Thureby on that day the following week. Beverley drove her home then, and the same thing happened several times, until when she lost her most profitable pupils a day or two before Christmas the disillusion came. Their mother, who was acquainted with Mrs. Nis- bet, spoke very plain words to her, and she left the house, knowing that the struggle for a living would henceforward be grimmer than ever. As it happened, she met Beverley in the motor as she walked home dejectedly, and when he stopped faced him with a little hard glint in her eyes. " I have nothing to say to you. Will you drive on?" she said.

Beverley got down from the car. "Of course, if you wish it. But I mean to know what has made this change in you. You didn't look at me that way two or three days ago." "No," said the girl, bitterly. " Still, I be- lieved you were what you told me you were then. Now I know you are one of the Hallow- stead Beverleys, and came over to marry Mi 66 Constance Nisbet."

A darker tinge crept into the man's bronzed face. 'Well, he said, almost sternly, "I want five minutes' straight talk with you."

" sorry I can't grant you them," said the girl, with a crimson spot in her cheek. "The reason ought to be very evident, and it is out of the question that I should peak to you again. If you insist upon stopping I shall go to those people yonder for protection." Beverley said nothing further, but, swing- ing off his cap, stepped back into the car and drove home thoughtfully, for he did not con- sider the affair by any means concluded so far as he was concerned. As it happened, he also met the man he had spoken with at the concert that night, and he Thad something of interest to say. "I'm not sure the girl who sings owes you very much," he said. "From what I heard o-day you have made it somewhat difficult for her to earn her living. Mrs. Nisbet is naturally rather looked up to by _Mrs. Cran- ston, and I believe Miss Lane got most of her pupils on the latter's recommendation. Don't you think it would be wiser if you. dropped that kind of thing?" "You mean that Constanee's mother would try to crush an innocent girl because I drove her home?" asked Beverley, sternly. The other man laughed. "Well," he said, "it really looks very like it." Beverley changed the subject. But he thought hard that night, and he • had what would have appeared to anybody else a wholly fantastic project in his mind when he drove over to Thurshy Grange on the next night, which, as it happened, WM Christmas Eve. Mrs. Nisbet had a good many guests at the Grange, and they had arranged an informal dance among themselves!. Beverley was evi- dently a little preoccupied. But he did his part, and when one waltz was over turned to his partner as they sat on the oak stairway in the big hall. "Who is the young man Constance Nisbet danced twice with?" he asked,

The girl looked at him with a little dry smile, for, ns it happened, she was not par- ticularly fond of Miss Nisbet. "Charley Fulke," she said. "He has a Lancer's commission, and practically nothing else.. That is, I believe, the reason he was not permitted to marry Constance, though she was supposed to be rather fond of him. That is, before—" She stopped suggestively, and Beverley looked down on her with a glint in his cyes. "You mean until I came on the scene. Still, you don't infer that Constar,ce is in any way fond of me?" The girt met his gaze steadily. She saw this most unconventional man, whom she rather liked, intended to have an answer. "It sounds a little dreadful. but, since you ask, Constance is rather afraid of her mother, and has made no great success of two town

Miss Lane looked at him steadily, and then made a little gesture. "She has gone to play for some children who are singing at a Christ- mas Eve supper in the school just outside the town. I'think it should be almost over now."

Beverley swung round with characteristic impetuosity, and it happened that when Maud .Lane came out from the school she found a car drawn up at the door. A man in a leather jacket swung her into it, and before she quite realised what had befallen her they had whirled out from among the gaslights into the open country. Then Beverley slowed down.

"Now," he said, "you and I have got to have a talk, and then, if you wish it, I will take you straight home again. You were rather nice to me as ,a sawmill engineer."

"Yes," said the girl, whose courge rose in face of the crisis, though there was a tremor in her voice. "I didn't' know then what manner of man you were." Beverley laughed a little. "Well," he said, " I'm just the same kind still, a sawmill en- gineer and nothing more, though I own the mill, and it's quite a big one. I'm also very badly in love with you, and have been since I saw you." Maud Lane made as though she would rise. "And you intended to marry Miss Nisbet all the time! The difficulty Is that you don't seem to understand that I am—"

Beverley seized her arm and held her mas- terfully. "I know just what you are, or I woaldn't want to marry you. What's more to the purpose, I'm going to." "But," gasped the girl, "that would cost you the Hallowstead property?" "I don't think it would. In fact, as you didn't seem to like me with it, I've just given most of my share of it to Miss Nisbet as a Christmas present. The girl laughed half hysterically. "You gave it away?" "I did. I'd have slung away every dollar if it had stood between you and me. Still— and your sister's coming out with us—the mill on the Fraser's going to keep all three quite comfortably. Now this thing's all fixed. I've got you, and when I get what I want I never let it go. All you have to do is to say Yes prettily." Maud Lane did not remember if she said anything at all, but she found herself drawn down bcside him in a seat that was not—quite big enough for two, while with a sudden hum- ming the car sped out into the open county/. Beverley laughed exultantly as he let the en- gine go at its utmost speed. "We're going to Thursby. There's quite a smart jeweller there," he said. " I hay() another Christmas gift to choose." "It's very late," said Maud Lane. "Will you want me too?" "Of course," said Beverley. "You see, it has to fit your finger."

eDuration.

So much attention has been THE WORK OF paid to the loss of the THE SESSION. Licensing Bill and the failure of the Education compromise that there has been a disposition to forget the work accomplished during the long Session of Parliament which has just closed. The House of Commons commenced its sittings before the first month of the year had expired, and it did not conclude theta until the first day of the present week. To the Government the loss of Sir HENRY CAMPBELL BANNERMAN was vary serious. He had brought the Liberal Party through lean times to the plentitude of office, and he was regarded by his followers with respect and affection such as can only be cultivated in adversity. Mr. ASQUITH, who by common con- sent was the natural successor of Sir HENRY, has intellectual qualifications to which his pre- decessor had not claim. But when he was called upon suddenly to exchange the office of Chan- cellor for that of head of the Government Mr. ASQUITH'S task was by no means light. Amongst the advanced Liberals he was regarded with some- thing approaching suspicion, and his cold, lawyer- like attitude in the House failed to secure for him that intimate human sympathy which assisted to bind Sir HENRY so closely to his party. In that sense, although his intellectual equipment is unquestionably greater than that of his prede- cessor, Mr. Asquinr was at a disadvantage which speedily disappeared. As head of the Government Mr. ASQUITH has sLewn that he possesses a very human side, and he has won over to him those who at first were disposed to regard him without enthusiasm. The Session in that sense has been a personal triumph for the PREMIER, despite the failure of two measures upon which he bestowed his own especial care. When we turn to the actual accomplishments in legislation of the Sassion, the list presented is certainly impressive. No less than seventy-one measures have been added to the Statute Book— forty-one before the close of the first portion of the session, and thirty as a resalt of the autumn sitting. Many of these are small bills, affecting what may be called minor interests. But they are none the less useful for that, and the credit of having put them into operation belongs legiti , mately to the whole House. Other measures are Id wider moment, affecting great principles and arge bodies of the public. Chief among these greater measures is the Old Age Pensions Act passed before the autumn recess. By • this measure half a million aged persons in ' the United Kingdom become entitled at the New . Year to a pension of ss. a week at the bands of the State. The sum is not large; it will in many oases do little more than keep the wolf from the

door. But it is a practical beginning in a direc- tion in which all parties have long desired to move. The condition of the aged poor in England, the misery to which old people who have lived res- pectably are often reduced, has been a standing reproach of the country. The Act passed during the Session may be, no doubt is, defective in many particulars, but it has laid a foundation upon which Parliament may work, and upon which may be erected a structure to supersede the barbarism of Poor-Law relief as it has been administered to the aged poor in the past. Well worthy of a place beside the Old-Age Pensions Scheme is the Act which has been passed for the protection of children. Legislation on these lines has long been desired. By the Act the lives of children will be made happier and safer than has been the case in the past, and something definite will be done to check the causes of physical deterioration in the rising generation which have been deplored by a succession of social reformers. Last, but not least, in the important measures of the Session comes the Eight Hours Bill for Coal Miners, a measure long demanded and strenuously resisted. No one who knows the condition of labour in coil mines will grudge the additional respite from work which this Act will give. It is to be hoped that the gloomy prognostications of an increase in the price of coal as a result of the Act will not be fulfilled. Looking at the Session as a whole, there is no cause, although there have been losses, to feel discouragement as to the power of Parliament to accomplish work for the nation. Even in the case of the Education Bill there has been substantial gain in understanding which may bear fruit when the House meets again.

To morrow the holiday will have begun. The .bustle of

To•MoltßOvV.

Christmas Eve will be over. The presents will have been received, the friends welcomed, and the festival of the dark days of the year will have come. The walls will have been decorated with holly, and the mistletoe hung in the ball. The tall yew tree will stand with its radiant burden in the hospital ward, and the red berries will gleam upon the window-sill in the church interior and the top at the stone columns. That is to say, these decorations will be in evidence with anything like good luck; but there are doubts about the sufficiency of some of the stocks and the sources of supply. To be told that there may not be enough mistletoe to go round is, unfortunately, in these commercial days not a convincing report. These announce- ments have come from Normandy before, and the goods have followed. More serious is the statement that berried holly is scarce this year. Last year saw abundance, and the Christmas before we were not ill-supplied ; but to go without the cheerful berry would be to lose the badge of the season, beside which the entire mistletoe bough could be lightly dispensed with. Whoever has got a generous show of "native coral-bright and armBd leaves" may count himself happy. Of Christmas trees there are enough to rejoice all the young hearts in England. That, at any tate, is good news. So long as the children are happy and the poor are fed, the festival of the children and of the poor, will have justified itself, and all earth's songs will have been set to music.

Local educationists who No Comritomisa. read the report of the pro- ceedings, last week, at the conference of clergy and lay representatives of he Rural Deanery of Winwick must have had their eyes opened to a considerable extent. The clerical members were very outspoken on the question of religious instruction—possibly they had forgotten the presence of the Press—and there cannot be the slightest doubt, now, in the minds of local Dissenters that a compromise is the last thing the clergy wish to entertain. They expressed satisfaction at the death of Mr. Runciman's , Bill, and had nor—sr-- word to any for ' the advances made by Noaconforrnists in r order to bring educational peace within the bounds of possibility. Nonconformist offers of , compromise are evidently considered by most of these men as confessions of weakness offering a sound inducement to the further stiffening of the clerical back. " Hold out, and we shall get all we want; is the favoured attitude. It was practically laid down at the conference of the Rural Deanery as the policy for the future. The Rev. E. E. Peters said " the time had come for the Church of England to take up a more offensive attitude, and not rely entirely on the defensive.' ,The clerical attitude is quite sufficiently offensive already in Nonconformist eyes. But if the Established Church is really anxious for a fight on the question of religious education in the day schools, we have no doubt that it can be readily accommodated, and perhaps get more fighting than it wants. The gage has been thrown down and no assenting educationist will hesitate tO pick it up and give battle when , and where the Church may please. But on the latter must rest the full responsibility of hindering education by casting it once more into the cock-pit of sectarian strife. - I Ir

The Rector of Warrington THE RECTOR'S was the only man present CANDOUR. who seemed to possess an atom of that sweet reason- ableness which one expects from ministers of the Gospel. He was refreshingly candid was the Rector, and said such nice things about M. Rtinciman's Bill Oa his brethren shivered appre- hensively. It did not suit them that Canon Willis should see good points in the attempted compromise and say as much publicly. For instance he said that" all children might have received religious instruction if the Bill had passed." No one attempted to deny this troublesome fact, 'lotrt subsequent speakers changed the subject from the dertd Bill as quickly as possible. Then the Rector went on to show that religious instruction was a much magni- fied essenti4l of the Church : "He was told by diocesan inspectors that there were parislies in which the clergy never went into their schools. They had been fighting about the right of entry into other schools and yet some clergy did not go into their own schools." This was also a very cold truth, and received anything but a warm and cordial reception from the Rector's brethren. The remarks of Canon Willis were certainly full of interest even if they caused disquiet in the minds of his hearers, and it strikes us as being a very remarkable coincidence that our con- temporaries should both publish a full report of the conference, but minus the Rector's views on the last Education Bill. Can this be the new or the neutral journalism ?

It is re-assuring to learn THE FENDING oF that not much more time SCHOOLICHILDREN. ia to be lost before the beneficent Act for the Provision of Meals to School Children is put into operation in Warrington. Nearly twelve months have been occupied in making the preliminary arangernents but now the machinery seems to be in working order and the supply of meals should begin early in the New Year. A little hitch occurred recently, the Town Council considering the prices charged for the necessary utensils to be extravagant and the consequence was that the list of purchases was referred back. The com- mittee however, adheres to its position, maintain- ing that the expenditure is quite reasonable, and at the monthly meeting on Monday some members of the Town Council who have als3 seats on. the Education Committee were taken severely to task for absenting themselves from Committee meetings, and reserving their criticisms for the Town Council. While the Council's power of revision is unquestioned, there is nevertheless a great deal to be said against this attitude. Very often an explanation at the Ethication Committee

meetings would suffice to remove any difficulty which suggested itself to the mind of a Councillor and obviate the necessity of raising the question before the Town Council. Of course, the directly elected representatives of the ratepayers are bound to insist upon economical administration and we should be very sorry if they neglected their duties in this respect. But at the same time, they should be careful not to lay themselves open to the charge of obstruction by failing to object at the committee meetings, where explana tions can be most conveniently given, and bringing the full force of their criticisms to bear when the matter reaches the Council. At first there was a probability that the Town Council's rejection of the proposal would mean a serious delay, but a special appeal to the Mayor and the Town Clerk, in view of the urgency of the case, has resulted in authority being given to pro- ceed with the work as speedily as possible, It is calculated that seven hundred Warrington children go to school under-fed,and we are glad that ere long there will be an end to the reproach that we compel them to receive instruction when they are not in a fit state to take full advantage of it.

Dr. Ambrose Shepherd's MEN IN TRW book, which is really on MAKING, character-building, raises some very serious reflections on the way in which many of the rising generation are growing up. Is it a fact that, for a large portion, our essentially English love cf sport is becoming a vice instead of a virtue ? Dr. Shepherd quotes the statement of a Bradford merchant : "I have five clerks in my office, who could prob- ably tell me all I want to know, and more, about a irace-horse or a cricket or football match, and not one of them could translate for me a foreign business letter." This is how one business man finds the workers under him. The author of the book relates the observation of a professor at a Scotch University as to a triumvirate of his pupils who were personalities there. They had a fine chance to make their mark in life ; but they hated "daily drudging,unwelcome work." There is an awful thought that this state may have been induced by the, training under the P ro fessor ; but we will hope not. The problem raised has often been discussed. We do no believe that, but for the sporting columns in the newspapers, our young men would lose the art of reading. The enormous sale of the popular sixpenny novels which is a thing of the last few years is an answer to that. They go among sell classes. There is the further fact to be remembered that many of the German youths who are held up to our admiration are very dull persons. The esprit de corps of the French is, perhaps, more to be emulated, though here is a danger, a distinguished philosopher said the other idly, of too much intellect. The tendency to indulge in intellectual reasoning and ideas may be carried too far. It is a platitude to say that we suffer from our insularity, and that our young people will be stimulated by travel. For those who cannot travel, the push ful alien comes to tell us why we fail to get on. He is a vary useful person. There is room for self-examination, but none for alarm.
