NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Two of our special Christmas features possess a certain amount of local interest. On page 2 is an article entitled " Dickens and the Christmas Ghost," written by Mr. George Edgar, a Warrington born journalist who at one time was in the service of the " Examiner." On page 3 Mr. Harold Bindloss, eldest son of Mr. E. W. Bindloss, formerly of Stockton Heath. contributes a short story entitled " Beverley's Christmas Gift." * * Many of our readers will be glad to learn that we have had news from Mr. Fred Stott, who is in Wellington, New Zealand. Writing under date, November Oth, he says : "It was a real pleasure, after being nine weeks without news, to read your paper. lam still looking for work. Trade is not very good, and there are plenty of unemployed." We hope we shall hear better news of Mr. Stott by the next mail.

* * * It is difficult to see what authority the French experts have for proclaiming as the "missing link" the recently unearthed gentleman whose remains show that, some 170,000 years ago, he used to walk on all fours, and was quite unable to smile, although his skull is distinctly a human one. "Consul" who is "alive and kicking" at Warrington to-day, has surely a better claim to the title of "missing link." * * . . _

Mr. Will Crooks told a good story relating to Mr John Burns in the House of Commons on Saturday. The President of the Local Government Board was addressing a meeting by the riverside, so close that the rising tide was wetting the feet of the audience. ' One man insisted on interrupting, until a stentorian voice exclaimed "Shall we chuck him in the river John " "Au, no ! said Mr. Burns, " I must remind you that under a section of the Local Government Act of 1889 it is a punishable offence to pollute a river." *.*.

There was a humorous incident connected with the recent stay of his Majesty the King at Brighton. The truth of the story is vouched for by a rev. gentleman who relates that a few days ago a boy walked up to his Majesty as he was walking along the esplanade at Hove and said to him, " Mister, can you tell me the time ? " " Yes," replied the King, taking out his watch. "It is a quarter to one." The boy then informed his Majesty that he had "been waiting two hours to see the blooming King," adding, " lam not going to wait any longer.' " Neither shall I," replied the King as he resumed his walk. His Majesty him- self afterwards related the incident with much gusto. * * In the debate upon Lord Robert Cecil's Public Meeting Bill—which may possibly deter some of the suffragette wreckers—Lord Newton observed that he supposed no bill ever passed with greater rapidity through the House of Commons since the Dynamite Bill. The official title of the bill referred to was the Explosive Substances Bill, and it was one of the panic bills of the coercion era. The trial of the Phoenix Park murderers, followed by an explosion at West- minster and the alleged discovery of a nitro-glycerine manufactory at Birmingham, induced Sir William Harcourt, who was then Home Secretary, in intro- ducing the bill on April 9, 1883, to ask the House to proced with all the stages of the bill at once. It was read the first and second times, considered in Com- mittee, and read the third time within two hours. Its passage was equally rapid through the House of Lords, and the Royal assent was given at noon on the following day.

* * * A ludicrous blunder perpetrated by the billposter has attracted some attention in Liverpool. The com- mittee of the Welsh Choral Union decided that cm the 27th March they would proiuce, for the first time in Liverpool, Granville Bantock's new work, " Omar Khayyam," with the following principals :—Miss Phyllis Lett, Mr. Philip Brozel, and Mr. Frederick Austin. Unfortunately, however, the billposter had not been sufficiently instructed as to the order in which he should display what—in the absence of any better description—may be described as his "sectional sheets." and his acquaintance with Persian literature being somewhat scanty, the result was startling. The hug,o poster announced, quite rightly, that on March 27th a performance would be given of Granville Bantock's new work, but added the surprising information that the principals would be Miss Phyllis Lett, Mr. Philip Brozel, and Mr. Omar Khayyam (first time in Liverpool)."

* * * A well-remembered Warrington men, Mr. Edward Porritt, who is writing the series of articles for the " Liverpool Daily Post and Mercury" on the revision of the United States tariff at Washington, has had the honour of being invited by President Eliot, of Harvard University, to deliver a special coarse of lectures on English history to the students of that famous American university. He will deliver about forty lectures in all, and the period dealt with will be that which lies between the end of the American Revolu- tion (1783) and the incoming of the Campbell- Bannerman Administration, in 1900. Mr. Porritt ia a journalist of wide experience, and received his early training at Warrington and has since worked in the Press galleries of the Legislatures at West- minster, Ottawa, and Washington. He has made a special study of modern English history, and the invitation to lecture at Harvard may be said to be the first public recognition of the work he did in writing the "History of the Unreformed House of Commons," an exhaustive work of two volumes. His most recent work is " Sixty Years of Protection in Canada," published last year, of which Macmillan's have undertaken to bring out a revised and extended edition.

A return of rates in a number of towns in the year ‹mding Mtrch next exhibits again what has been going on now for several years—the tendency for local charges to rise in amount. Five boroughs— East Ham, Halifax, Norwich, Swansea, and Wolver- hampton pay between Ss. and Os. in the £, that in the eastern city being only 2cl. below half a sovereign. Rates of over Ss. In the £ are levied at Bacup, Brighouse, Dewsbury, Hull, Huddersfield, Lceds, Manchester, Middlesborogh, Northampton Salford, Southampton, Stafford, Wakefield, Wigan, and Preston. There are many more in the is to Ss. list, including Warrington with exactly 7s. Oxford is the lowest rated in over seventy largo boroughs, the charge there being only 49. 51, a drop of 3d. in the £ on last year. Lancaster (43. 101) is the only ()Vier town under :ie.

* * * The Rev. A. 1/. Mitchell, Vicar of Buttonwood, is nothing if not downright in his criticisms. His latest tirade, published in the Buttonwood Parish Magazine, is delivered against the time-honoured practice of Christmas feasting. Possibly it is less abused now than ever it was, but this does not lessen the vigour of the Vicar's denunciations: "'Eat' is the motto of the world's greatest birth- day and the days which follow ; 'eat, eat on, eat more, rat ever, eat till you can eat no more '—and then? Why, as soon as possible, begin to gorge again. Happiness is in proportion to appetite ! Ceasing to eat you limit enjoyment and restrain happiness ; such at least is the law of the mince pie." The vicar is shocked especially to think that "all altars must give praer to the kitchen altar at Christmastide. Moloch must be first, Christ may be second, probably third." * * * From his position as chairman of the Conservative Club, Councillor Fulton-Smith has A right to instruct " the members politically. He gave them a lesson on Tuesday evening, in the course of which he said' Tho Government have done very little during the past session." We do not quarrel with him on this account, but we cannot help reflecting on the difference the point of view makes. If one conveni- ently forgets half the Government has done, and calls the other h ilf useless, it is natural to conclude that the Government has done "very little." This is the style of argument which satisfies many a " stout Conservative." Councillor Fulton-Smith also said that Socialism was "-the ultimate goal the Govern- ment had been aiming at." Well, the Socialists do not think so or they would not split the Liberal vote at the by-elections hnd put the Tory in. The Coun- cillor may be a good club chairman but he handles his politicil cue clumsily.

LOCAL NEWS.

Attempted Suicide. About six o'clock on Tuesday evening Alfred Rickhuss (aged 45), residing at 43, Heath-street, Stockton Heath, was admitted to the Infirmary suffer- ing from self-inflicted injuries to his throat. He is not expected to recover.

Death of Miss Boyle. Many of our readers will regret to hear of the death of Miss E. Boyle, who passed away at the Infirmary on Sunday morning. The deceased lady was admitted to the institution at her own earnest request on December 14th, Dr. G. W. Joseph, her medical attendant, making the necessary arrangements. Miss Boyle was for many years a most diligent worker on behalf of the Parish Church, and she also gave mucl, valuable help to such worthy objects as the Infirmary Women's Collection Fund, and the Infirmary Bazaar. PUBLIC BENRFIT UNDER. TUN CLOCK FORBEST BOOTS IN LANCASII UM—Advt. Taxation of Land Values. At their December meeting the members of the Warrington Branch of the English League fer the Taxation of Land Values passed the following resolu- tion : " That this League expresses the hope that the Government will take the earliest opportunity of introducing a sound measure of Land Reform by taxing land values in accordance with pledges already given both as a means of raising revenue and of dealing with the problem of unemployment by pro- moting a more profitable use of the land, and that copies of this resolution be sent to the Prime Minister, the Chanceller of the Exchequer and our Member."

Secondary School Re-Union. Complete success attended the second annual re-union of old students of the Warrington Secondary School and Pupil Teachers' Centre, which was held at the school on Saturday evening. There was a gather- ing of old pupils to the number of about 150, from places as far distant as London and Dundee, and staff and students spent a very enjoyable evening. A short farce, played by five of the older staldents, opened the evening and was followed by whist and dancing. After the presentation of whist prizes by the Principal (Jr. E. S. A.. Robson), the proceedings came to an end at 11 p.m., with the dancing of "Sr Roger de coverley," and the singing of " Auld Lang Syne."

The Examiner Thursday, December 24, 1908.

Alleged Housebreaking. At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, morning, James Towey, labourer, of no settled abode, was charged with breaking and entering the dwelling- house of William Allman, 52, Allen-street, on Saturday night, with intent to commit a felony. - The Chief Constable said the prisoner was fortunately discovered in the house More he could steal anything. In all probability other charges would be preferred against Lim, and a remand was necessary. —Prisoner was remanded for eight days.—Michael Moran,ironworker, of no settled abode, was also remanded in connection with breaking and entering the premises of William Furnival, Bewsey-street, on Saturday night. Prisoner, to gain an entrance, smashed a pane of glass, the noise of which caused the house dog to bark and break loose which attractad a little girl, who informed the prosecutor. CHRISTMAS CHORAL SERVICES.

WYCLIFFE CLIIIRCE.

A Christmas Choral service was held at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday evening when selec- tions from "The Messiah " were given by the choir assisted by a number of friends. There was a good congregation and it was the general opinion that the music was beautifully interpreted and reflected the greatest credit upon all concerned. The soloists were heard to great advantage. Miss J. Armstrong sang "0 Thou that tellest," " He shall feed His flock" and " He was despised and rejected," very expressively, and Miss A. Griffiths acquitted herself well in "There were Shepherds" and "Rejoice greatly." Mr. F. Brown sang" But who may abide," "The people that walked in darkness" and " Why do the nations" with fine effect, and Mr. A. Griffiths' pure-toned voice found good scope in "Comfort ye" and "Every valley." The choir did full justice to the choruses •4 And the glory," "For unto us a Child is born," " Glory to God," All we like sheep," " Lift up your heads " and " Hallelujah." ST. PAUL'S CHURCH.

A musical service was held at this church on Sunday evening, when the choir gave an admirable rendering of "Noel,' a church cantata for the festival of Christmas, the words being by Samuel Bath and the music by Ernest A. Dicks. There was a large congre- gation. Mr. E. Wilkes presided at the organ, and the soloists were Miss Armstrong, Mrs. Cottrell, Mr. C. F. Roberts (Liverpool Cathedral), Mr. Leonard Aukland (who formed the quartette party), and Mr. E. Walker. Mr. Roberts was suffering from a cold, bat he sang well especially in "Comfort ye,"which was given by special request at the conclusion of the cantata. The solos and choruses were all most creditable and the members of the choir are to be congratulated upon their excellent work.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Immense interest has been aroused in Warrington by the visit of Consul, the famous chimpanzee, to the Palace and Hippodrome. Consul is described asi the "almost man," and the" missing link " which proves the Darwinian theory of evolution. Certainly he is the most remarkable and highly trained ape which has ever been exhibited before the public. There can be little doubt, that, in addition to his alrn )st human appearance, Consul is the possessor of a brain which , is capable of demonstrating a certain amount of intelligence. To see this monkey sit at table in dress , clothes and eat with the ordinary implements in quite a well-behaved fashion is a sight not to be readily forgotten. Consul finishes the meal with a ' cigarette, which he lights and consumes with the air of a connoisseur. Among his other feats are dressing and undressing, and the riding of a tricycle. Even if he cannot talk,Consul can make a noise exceedingly like a child's laugh. Delighted crowds have been the order at the Hippodrome this week, and the young chimpanzee is quite a favourite. The other turns are rather eclipsed, but they contribute to an attractive programme. Rastus and Banks, the coloured entertainers, are a popular pair. Rastus does some remarkable dancing, and his lady partner some pleasing singing. Margaret Radcliffe is again to the fore with popular illustrated songs, the choruses of which goes with a swing Captain Devereux introduces an accomplished troupe of dogs, who appear in a unique representation of the streets of London including the arrest of a huge canine beggar by a diminutive doggy policeman. Johnny Holland and Co. again present a laughable sketch, the title this week being "My Friend Boots" in which Mr. E. W. Edmonds, Miss Rita Warne, and Mr. John Holland appear. The latter is the life and soul of the piece, and as the boots-policeman is Iscreamingly funny. An uncommon item is provided by the Primrose Comedy Four, a qoartette of male part singers who appear in a " Song Scena " Their voices blend very harmoniously, and some of the vocal numbers were very cordially received. Lily Arthur , pats plenty of go into her songs and dancing and bids fair to become an accomplished cemedienne.

There will be a grand matinee with fall stir com- pany at 2.30 on Boxing Day. NEXT WEEK'S PRO3RAMIE.

1 Another attractive programme is announced for 'next week. The bill is headed by Dolly Elsworthy, " The Orango Girl "an other artistes will be t Hyde and Cody Trio, ecaentrics in unique tricks ; The Walton% eoaentric gymnasts ; Julian Ross, conaedian ; Roe and 'Vold, ia comedy wire act ; Betson's comedy cyclists in " & tramp's holiday Johnny Dwyer, the versttile comedian.

The minutes were approved. A LUNATIC'S LEGACY. REMARKABLE DISCUSSION.

At a meeting of the House Committee the Charm reported that Mr. W. Hickman had repaid to the solicitor acting on behalf of Sergeant Richards, an inmate of the Workhouse, the whole of the money he had received from Richards' estateand, if the Guardians were prepared to receive this sum, the solicitor was prepared to pay to the Guardians a cheque for this amount.—lt was moved by Mr. EVANS seconded by Mr. W. C. BROOKS, that the Clerk be instructed to receive, on behalf of the Guardians, 4 cheque for the amount referred to.—For, 4 votes ; against, 5 votes.—The CHAIRMAN declared the motion lost. _

-- The CHAIRMAN moved and Mr. JOLLEY seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. A. BROoK3 asked the Clerk to make a statement with regard to the repayment mentioned above. It seemed to him that a question like that should not go through without on explanation to the full Board. It looked like smugggling through what to some of the House Committee seemed to be a grave offence. Although the House Committee had done nothing but what every member of the Board would have done if he bad had to deal with it—it was perhaps done ont of good nature—it seemed to him that a public body like the Board should be put in possession of the full facts before a minute like that was confirmed. The CLERK said information came to him a month or so ago that an inmate of the Workhouse named Sergeant Richards had come into some money in February last, at a time when he was chargeable to the Union as a lunatic and detained in the Workhouse Infirmary. He made inquiries and found that the man was entitled to a sum of £52 10s. 2d. under the will of a relative who had died near Wolverhampton.He was also informed that the money was in the hands of Mr. Hickman, of Warrington. He interview6d Mr. Hickman who told him that he had the money and that he would be prepared to pay to the Guardians the balance he had left after taking out certain moneys which were due to him from the man, He (the Clerk) told Mtn that he was not prepared at the time to iteCept the balance from him and, acting on the instructions of the House Committee, whose resolution was approved by the Board, he went further into the matter' with the result that he was approached by a solicitor who told him he WAs acting on behalf of Mr. Hickman and that the amount which he had received in the first instance had been repaid by hint to Sergeant Richards' estate. He (the Clerk) brought it before the House Committee at the last meeting and asked them if they were prepared to receive the money but, according to the resolution the Committee were not prepared to receive that sum, The CHAIRMAN : What surn ig that? The CLERK: The sum Mr. Hickman has repaid and which he received is about £44. The full amount is £52 10s. 2d. but he did not receive the full amount— only £4B odd, which is minus the solicitors' expenses of obtaining the money. Miss BROADBENT said she did not quite understand this business. She moved an amendment that the money he accepted. Mr. R. LER seconded. Mr. BOLTON was also in favour of accepting the money. Mr. JOLLEY contended that the resolution of the House Committee was a reasonable and proton one. The members of the Committee expressed themselves extremely dissatisfied with the events which had led up to this.. It was perfectly clear to the members of the Committee that Mr. Hickman had no right whatever to any portion of the money and the Committee declined to accept any portion of the money less than the whole amount,whatever excuses were offered for the pronosed deduction from it. Mr. Brooks appeared to think it was open to grave doubt whether the Board as a public body ought to accept the money at all in the circum- stances, and whether they ought not to take steps by legal process to recover the money, as custodians of this unfortunate old man who bad been there as a certified lnnatio for a long time and whose property was in the possession of Mr. PHickman. The process by whidh Mr. Fliokman obtained possession of that property he could not speak about, because had no direct knowledee of it. Several state- ments had been made in an informal way but it was perfectly clear that by no proper process could property of a certified lunatic under thecontrol of the Guardians pass into the hands of arty individual. The resolution did not say that the matter was at an end. What the House Committee said was that they refused to make terms. The Clerk was instructed to demand the pay- ment of the whole of the money to which this poor old man was entitled and that action had been confirmed by the Board. It would be lamentable that a public body should descend to haggle about a matter of £3 or £4 when the issue at stake was not a matter of money but of principle. Miss BROADBENT : If we went through with the first resolution, it would implicate the solicitor. The CHATRM AN : It is not our business. The Rev. M. Ryas, as one who dissented from Mr. Jolley, pointed out that the committee was not any- thing like unanimous in the matter.He believed that in taking charge of the money Air. Hickman was acting as the man's agent. Whatever legal expenses had been incurred by Mr. Hickman would have been equally incurred if the money had been first acquired by the , Guardians. He did not think the Board would suffer in I the least by accepting the offer and he did not see where the principle came in.Tt would settle the matter and let sleepings dogs lie. He was afraid the Board had not had a good name lately and this would only make it worse.

tfr. EvAss said the main point of difference in the Committee was with regard to the expenses of the solicitor. It was not altogether against the principle, that they vote.-1, but against the redaction of the amount. Mr. Jolley's position was that the Board should not sacrifice what principle was involved in the matter,

_ The CHAIRMAN : Mr. Hickman has already handed the money hack to the estate—the whole of it. The CLErm : All that he had, Mr. EVANS : Do Vol mean to say Mr. Hickman is prepared to give us £52 103. instead of £43 10s. The CLERK: No. Mr. Hickman has never had .C 52 105. He has paid back to the solicitor the whole of the money he has received. Mr. EVANS said that as there was so little difference he did not see that it would he worth while to contend for principle and spend more of the money. Were the Guardians justified in spending money in getting the amount through legal channels ? He presumed they could not get it without. Mrs. COLLTNOwOOO : How was it Mr. Hickman got the money at all ? is it not the fault of the 11 ATI of Guardians that they did not get the money, if the man is in their charge ? Mr. ANVIIERY (master) at the request of the Chairman stated what be knew of the case. So far as his recollec- tion served him, some months ago Mr. Hickman came to the Workhouse and made a personal application to have the old man Richards out for a few hours, stating that he would be responsible for him and bring him back. He believed Mr. Hickman Said he wanted the man on a little private business. He (Mr. AwberY) readily consented to the application and Mr. Hickman took the.man and -bronght him back again. He subse- quently came to see him on two or three occasions and brought him some tobacco. No mention was ever made of Richards having had some money left to him, so far as his personal knowledge went, and he (Hr. Awbery) had no idea the man had money. The CLERK said that when he saw Mr. Hickman he told him that he had been waiting for the right people to call upon him for the money. Mr. A. BROOKS said the point on which the gravity of the whole thing rested was : Did the master think Mr. Hickman knew the man was a certified lunatic? Mr. AWRERY : That lv cannot answer. lie knew he was in the lunatic.ward. Mr. ROBERT LEE said he understood Mr. Hickman had acted as Richards' agent in the whole matter. Mr. BROOKS : A man cannot act on the signature of a lunatic.

_ Mr. EVANS said he believed Mr. Hickman was acting in the capacity of trustee. Mr. La R : He did not know the man was a lunatic. _ _

Mr. iiimANcE suggested that it would be in the interests of all concerned if Mr. Hickman could appear before the House Committee. gost of the members bad a knowledge of Mr. Hickman and they believed he would do the right thing. Unfortunately Mr. Hickman had been there and found a person chargeable to the Guardians and apparently had not the presence of mind to report the fact to the principal officer of the Guardians that the man was the possessor of a legacy and apply to the Guardians as to what should be done with reference to his chargeability Mr. FlvAss admitted it was an unfortunate circum- stance that Mr. Hickman did not apply to the I Guardians. He. however, thought it would be better to accept the offer made the committee. The money would not increase by any other action they might take.

CHRISTMASTIDE. _— Local Preparations for the Festivities.

There is nothing particularly "Chrisimassy " in the atmosphere as yet, and the prospects are that the traditional weather will be left entirely to the imagination this Yuletide. But if the "old-fashioned" conditions are conspicuously lacking, the appearance or the streets speaks eloquently of the approach of the great festival. The shop windows are a blaze of light and colour and the tradesmen have vied with each other in arranging the most alluring displays of seasonable wares of all descriptions. They have gone to an infinite amount of trouble and no doubt their enter- prise will meet with its due reward. Christmas is pre-eminently a home festival, and there will be few abodes, however lowly, to which the joys of the festive season will not penetrate. Those whose lot it is to spend the day in any of our public institutions will also participate in the rejoicings to some degree. Christmas Day finds the sombreness of the Work- house relieved with tasteful decorations, and naturally the occasion is looked forward to with pleasure for many weeks by the inmates. An abundance of Christmas faro is provided, and some idea of the magnitude of the catering can he gained from the fact that the food to be consumed includes 5301bs of beef, 6001bs. of pudding and 400Ibs. of cake. The inmates of the House number 619, and of the hospital 203, the total being about 109 more than last Christmas. Each man is allowed one pint and each woman half a pint of beer. For those who do not take beer mineral waters are provided. In the evening there will be a Christmas tree for the children, and the usual smoking concert will be held in the dining hall for the adults. Gifts of evergreens have been received from Mr. J. Charlton Parr, Grappenhall Heyes ; Mrs. Rylands, Massey Hall, Thelwall ; Sir Gilbert Greeaall, Bart., Walton Hall ; and Mrs. Broadbent, The Hollies, Latchford ; toys from the Mayor and Mayoress, the children of Fairfield Higher Grade School, and fancy sweets from Mr. Seton. At Padgate Cottage Homes the children will engage in the usual festivities, concluding with a concert in the evening. The Mayelf (Alderman Forslaaw) has kindly arranged for the distribution of hot-pot and mince pies to the deserving poor at the Drill Hall between 11 and 12 nom. It is particularly requested that thoee who apply for the food will bring suitable receptacles. In addition, the Mayor bas shown kindly consideration for several of the military veterans who are in poor circumstances and to day they will be the recipients of Christmas hampers. Christmas is always the busiest time of the year at the Post Office and to-morrow morning the pastrnan will be seen staggering beneath his burden of Christ- mas missives and piesents. Excellent arrangements have been made to cope with the rush and the accommodation at the old Post Office, as well as the handsomely appointed new buildings, is being utilized.

PENKETH.

BIBLE CrAss SocriL.—The Penketh Wesleyan Men's Bible Class annual social was held in the Wesleyan Sunday Schools, on Saturday evening last. AN OPPONENT FOR MR. STEPHEN WALSU,M.P

Mr. Walter Greaves Lord. of Southport, was invited at a meeting at Wigan on Monday, to contest the Ince division of Lancashire in the Unionist interest at the next election. The sitting member is Mr. Stephen Walsh (Labour). Mr. Greaves Lord is a son of the late Mr. Simeon Lord, who was formerly a cotton , spinner and manufacturer' at Platt Bridge, in the Inca division. He was educated at Wigan Grammar School, and afterwards at University College, Liverpool, and graduated at Victoria University with the degree of L.L.S. in 1891 lie CMS Arden Scholar of Grays Inn in 1900, and was called to the Bar the same year. fie joined the Northern circuit. He was chairmln of the Liverpool Junior Conservative Club in 1907-8, and is hon. secretary of the Southport Division Conservative Association.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

LABOURER'S SUDDEN DEATH.

James Furlong, a dock labourer (50), died in a lodging-house in Marsh-street, Widnes,early on Tuesday morning. After supper, on Monday evening, Furlong retired to bed about ten o'clock, but upon the lodging- house attendant going to his bed to arouse him to go to work, life was found to be extinct. Information was at once given to the police, and the body was conveyed to the mortuary.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES UNEMPLOYED.

According to the promise of the Highway Committee to try and find employment for some of the men who are out of work through depression in trade, a great I number presented themselves at the Town Hall on Monday, when the Mayor (Councillor Tom Sraitb) intimated that work could only be found at present for a small number of men. It took some time to pick out the most deserving cases, those with the largest families having preference, and twenty-four were given work at slag breaking, twelve at street repairinE, and six at the gasworks, and as many as could be found work to paint street signs. It was expected that the Post Office work of laying the new cable would be opened on Monday, but orders have been received from London that the work was to lay in abeyance until after Chrisrmas. This news caused disappointment to many of them.

LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY ALTERATIONB.

The Clerk read a letter from the Commons and Foot- paths Preservation Society stating that in connection Iwith the London and North-Western Railway Bill statutory notice had been given of the intention of the promoters to seek power to acquire land for railway j purposes within the area Ender the jurisdiction of the Council and asking the Council to secure an undertaking from the company for the erection of swing gates, bridges, subways, stiles and other convenient means of passage where the lines crossed any public carriage ways, bridle ways or footpaths. The Clerk said the plans deposited by the company only showed a small widening at Sutton Weaver and it I would not affect the Council.

Trig HEALTH OF. FRODSHAm.

As requested by the Council, Dr. id-a-rm—e presented a special report on infectious diseases In the parishes of Frodsham and Frodsham Lordship. It showed that during the past four years there had been in the whole rural district 47 cases of typhoid fever and three deaths but not a single case occurred in Frodsham or ' Frodsham Lordship. The scarlet fever cases numbered I ' , 593 with three deaths and in Frodsharn there were 93 cases with three deaths, and in Frodsham Lordship, 19 • cases and no deaths. Of diphtheria there were 25 cases i with 30 deaths, and in Frodsham there were 37 cases with five deaths and in Frodsham Lordship 14 cases and Ino deaths. The average number of cases to the popula- tion in the Frodsham parishes differed to no i unfavourable extend from the remainder of I the district. It was the fate of every • district in its turn to have a bad year or period and the present time was that of the Frodsbam area. He could not point to any specific cause of the outbreak. Observations as to alleged inefficient drainage and sewerage arrangements had been made, but in the absence of any specific complaint or of specific know- ledge on his part of the alleged defects, he would be exceeding his duty if he went further than to convey to the Council the observations addressed to him. Mr. Linaker said he was very gratified to find that Frodsham did not differ from any other part of the district. There had been a great deal of talk as to alleged unhealthy conditions in the parishes, but the report did not bear out that contention. The report was adopted. VICTORIA-ROAD, G RAPPRNII ALL. The report was adopted. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of I Guardians was held at the Dutton Workhouse on , Tuesday afternoon, Mr. T. Baxter presiding. OLD AGE PENSIONSI AND OUT-RRLIEF.

The Cl;r—k—prOduced a return of persons_over 70 years i of age in receipt of outdoor relief n the Union. The figures were :—Rdneorn district,Bl; Frodsham district, 152 ; Budsvorth district, 42. I Mr. Uallows suggested that the matter be brought before a committee, so that they could speak their minds. It was a very important thing. The Chairman: It is too big a question to go into at this meeting. Mr. Woodward asked if it was really worth while discussing the question? They had had a semi-official statement to the effect that in the immediate future these cases were to be rernoved from the hands of , Boards of Guardians and were to become eligible for ; old-age pensions in conjunction with those who were , already eligible. They had that statement from a , responsible member of the Cabinet and therefore he did not think it was neoessary to spend time discussing the question. The Chairman : It would be advisable to wait and ; see what is decided. .

Mr. Hallows said there would be important results in this matter. These people would eventually become eligible for pensions but if the Guardians went on adding to their income they might not get the PS nsions. Mr. Davies said that in the Frodsham district alone if all those over seventy received 3s. a week it would make a difference of .C2DO per year. Mr. Haining said what gave rise to th 3 report being asked for, was that Mr. Stokes a fortnight ago said such pitiful,cases came before the Pensions Committee that he was compelled to bring the matter before the Board so that they could go into the whole of the conditions and give small increases of relief where necessary. The proposal was not that all should receive ss. a week. Mr. Woodward said every man who had served on the Relief Supervision Committee know that every case was taken on its individual merits. It was carefully con- sidered and the Guardian representing the district from which the case came was at liberty to make any suggestions for an increase if he thought it was necessary. Mr. Spruce said the relieving officers kept in touch with all these people and on their recommendation the Relief Supervision Committee from time to time increased the amounts allowed. Ile did not think there was any harm done under the present arrangement. The Chairman : lam not sure whether it won't be an evil day n hen we treat the deserving and undeserving alike and give them all Is. a week. A voice : Would it include loafers ? The Chairman : If you must treat everybody alike when they are over seventy, you must give to loafers and everybody. I think the worst of them ought to be forced to come here' instead of giving everybody 55., a week whether they deserve it or not. They get it if they go before the Old Age Pensions Committee and we cannot help ourselves. If they are very deserving we have cases where we give considerably over ss. aweek Mr. Linaker proposed that the papers lie on the table and be kept for future reference. The Chairman said it seemed as if their relieving officers had been put to a great deal of unnecessary trouble and he was not sanguine that they were not going to do very great injury. They were going to destroy thrift altogether if they were going to give Is. a week to people whether they were deserving or not. Mr. Linaker, jun. : We are not giving it at present. The Chairman : No, we are not at present, but this seems to be a proposition to give public money away to everybody. Mr. Ilaining : I think you are making statements in error. There was no such proposition. Continuing he slid that the object of the inquiry was not to give everybody Is. a week but in some of the worst cases of human suffering to give a little extra relief. Ile knew a case at Runcorn of a lady who had led a pure and blameless life and who had been receiving relief for about eighteen months. Yet unless some alteration were made, her relief would remain the same next year while some of her neighbours, whose conduct might not bear comparison with hers, were receiving ss. It was in cases like that that some increase should be given. Mr. Norman said that if Mr. }Lining was deeply interested in a case and thought the woman deserved more he had the opportunity to plead her cause before the Relief Supervision Committee and he did not. think there would be much opposition to his wishes. He (Mr. Norman) had every sympathy with the feeling that it seemed hard on those who bad been receiving

5

parish relief that they shonld be excluded from the old age pensions, but he was sure this would soon be put. right. The Chairman : It will right itself if you wait a few years. Mr. Linake's proposition was agreed to. TUBERCULOSIS.

CHRISTMAS _RELIEF.

The Clerk of the Chorlton Union reported as follows: The Guardians of this Union have decided to allow to their out-door poor extra relief to the extent of is. per case for the week including Christmas Day. To secure uniform treatment this additional amount will also be paid to all cases relieved in this Union on behalf of other Unions. The Guardians further approve the pay- ment to their non-resident poor of such additional relief as may have been decided on in each Union respectively. The Guardians of Barrow-in-Furness Union also intimated that they had adopted a similar course. A NURSE'S RESIGNATION.

The Clerk intimated that Nurse Croughton had sent. in her resignation and her duties would terminate on January 14th. The resignation was accepted and it was decided to advertise for a successor.

RELIEVING OFFICER'S SALARY.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee Mr. J. Pearson's application for an increase of his salary as relieving officer was considered and it was recommended that an advance from £9O to £lOO be granted. The minutes were confirmed. TRRATM ENT OF PIITIIISIS. Mr. Woodward reported on the proceedings at a conference held in connection with the North-Western Poor Law Conference to consider the treatment of cases of phthisis. A pro- position was made that there should be notification of all cases of phthisis and that provision should be made by the Guardians for the detention acd care of all cases notified. He moved an amendment that the time was not come when Boards of Guardians should under- take any further expenditure in behalf of phthisioal patients in their districts and strongly recommending that cases coining into the workhouses should be strictly isolated. The amendment was, however, defeated. A Committee was appointed to take a further view of the situation and he was elected a member. It was a matter which must be very carefully watched as It might involve the Guardians in a heavy expenditure which would at present be useless.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ST. JOHN'S MISSION EIALL.

Last Thursday night at the Mission Hall, Logh-street, a service entitled" Song and light," was given by the choir and children under the direction of Mr. T. Martindale. The piece opens with a stately chorus by GO girls, supplemented by the choir, " Sing glory, suitable Scriptural responses being read by Miss Kate Drakefield, who presided. Then followed an exercise "Children of Christmastide," by Maggie Watkinson (" hope "), Amelia Barrett (" Joy,") Ethel Twist (" Peace "), Nellie Halton (" Goodwill"), and Grace Seddon ("Love"). After the chorus " Sung and light " was an exercise by 18 children, who enter to slow music, holding letters in their tight hands. Those whose letters spell " Christ " step forward then a second lot show "mas," the word being thus completed by effective movements. After the full chorus Capt. tlaslam gave an address. Ethel Thane sang the "Angels' Song," Alice Martindale recited "0 dreaming world," and the chorus "0 Judah, take thy harp" followed. Alice Hancock, Gladys Roberts, Bessie Corrin, Alice Hilton, and Lucy Hill came forward, nicely dressed in white. Florrie Marsh recited "Is he a

king ? " May Caunce sang" It is Christmas in heaven," Esther Bailey " Shine on," and Polly Watkinson recited "North wind and fir tree." There are various other choruses, and the piece concluded with " Send forth the evangel." The service was very brightly and satisfactorily rendered.

CONCERT IN AID OF THE N.S.P.C.C. A concert was given in Newton Town Hall, last I Thursday night, in aid of the funds of the above most deserving society. There was a very large and 11 appreciative audience, and at the outset Mr. E. A. White I (local correspondent for the Society) made a few remarks, concluding by expressing thanks to Misses Hartley and Peck for the great trouble they had gone to in arranging the entertainment; he also thanked the audience and the performers. A. very attractive programme opened with two part songs by the pupils, ' Gentle Swallow " (Macbeth), and" Where the bee sucks" (Dr. Arne), in which there was a very agreeable blending of tone. Hilda Davenport recited a pretty story concerning " Flo's letter," and the action song "The Dunces ' (Foxwell) proved a most diverting item, the humour of the piece being well brought out. Irene and Marjorie Tunstall gave a very clever Dutch dance in costume. Each item was encored. The feature of the first part was a sketch by F. W. Adams, entitled "The three Fairy Gifts." The Fairy Queen, Titania, was personated by Edith James, other , fairies by Doris Anderton, Gerty Goslin, Doris Stratton, ' and Dollie Edwards; the sisters Cynthia (Eileen t hake), Violet (Hilda Sutton), and Vent /orothy Ormand), who are set upon by robbers, Itozahel White and Helen liaison. Edith James, as the Fairy Queen, deserves a. special word of praise, while excellent all-round support was accorded by the other pupils. The second part opened with an intelligent rendering of a pianoforte solo of Moszkowski's by Gladys Ormand. " The new baby "was capitally sung by Queenie Pugh and Dorothy Ormand, and secured a hearty recall. The Frivolity " dance was a very graceful execution by Irene Tunstall, and had to be repeated. A musical tableau followed, three charming stage pictures being presented, Viz. : " Doctor's Visit," " Darby and Joan,' and " Sleeping Beauty," the effect being greatly heightened by the suitable songs rendered by unseen voices. The last item was a selection from Shakes- peare's " Taming of the Shrew,,' the characters being well-sustained as follows :—Baptista (Gladys Ormand), Lucentio (May Langton), Petruclaio (Millie Mouucey), Geuraio (Gertrude Stratton), Curtis ( Marjorie Tunstall),, Katharina (Lrene Tunstall), Bianca (Rozabel White).

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS PASSIVE RESISTERS*

At St. Helens on Monday, the annual band of passive resisters was summoned. The defendants were Or. T.T. Best, Rev. W. F. Jeffries (Congregational), Mr. W. Barker, Rev. J. Bradley (Primitive Methodist), Mr. J. B. Anderton, Rev. J.G wilym Jones (Congregational), Mr. Thomas Jenkinson' J.P., Rev.W.Holroyd (Baptist), and the Rev. Jos. W. Dickenson (Congregational). The Rev. J. Bradley was the only defendant who appeared. The order was made. "A CHAPLAIN AT THE FRONT." INTERESTING ST. HELENS CHURCH PROJECT The Rev. Cyril Bardsley, vicar oi St. lielens,speaking at the men's Bible class, ou Sunday afternoon, said that the parochial council had decided not to appoint a chaplain for the Bible class, but to send an extra mount to the Church Missionary Society to pay for a missionary who would practically be their chaplain at the front' in the great battle with heathendom. Mre Bardsley said it was intended to raise an extra amount each year as nearly as possible equal to the stipend of a curate, which would be forwarded to the Missionary Society. The Ven. Archdeacon Madden,who delivered an impres- sive address on" The Day of Judgment," prefaced it by commending the action of the parish in this matter, and said that toe sacrifice they were making was in the right spirit. It would do their souls good. The religion of Christ was not selfish—not a religion to " get " but a religion to" give." Be had been stirred by what he had heard from their vicar, and he knew that the Bishop was most thankful that they should have acted so unselfishly and so splendidly in that matter.

A BENEFACTOR To C.kTS.—Dr..Tean Danysz, Director of the Laboratory of Agricultural Micro-Biology, at the Pasteur Institute, Paris, whose name is identified with a well-known remedy against the depredations of rats and mice, kas just made a discovery of interest to all lovers of cats. Every winter for some years past there has been a serious epidemic amongst cats in the Department of Euro in Normandy, which proved parti- cularly deadly to kittens. Last February, large number of cats suocumbed to the disease after only a few. day illness. One of the victims was despatched to Dr. Danysz' Laboratory in Paris, and an autopsy was per- formed. This enabled Dr. Damysz to produce and cultivate the microbe of the mysterious disease. Later, he produced a serum which, as an anti-toxin, renders " puss "entirely immune to the dreaded malady. A treatise on the subject of this latest scientific discovery is included in a recent number of the " Annalcs de L'lmatitut Pasteur."

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist." I believe there is no truth in the rumour that Warrington have signed on Consul, as understudy to Jack Fish.

Nevertheless there should be considerable interest in the week-end matches, seeing that Warrington meet two teams who have had the temerity to beat them earlier in the season, and fully expect to wipe out the stain.

The Australians sustained their sixth defeat, at Wakefield, who are one of the smartest club sides. I am glad Warrington hare renewed fixtures with the Trinitarians. My last acquaintance with them was on the occasion of Warrington's appearance at Bell Vue. Wakefield, in the Northern Union Cup. Warrington lost by a goal kick, after having all the best of the game, and missing glorious chances.

A; r

Leigh are expecting to be accompanied to Warring- ton by a large crowd of their supporters. Numerous changes have been made in the Leigh team, which was chosen on Monday night. Lavery, the New Zealander, is to play outside half, his colleague be:ng Woodward Whittaker, the regular half, having been injured at Broughton. Another trial will accordingly be given at centre-tbreequarters to Leah, a native of Wai rington,wbo played well in the Oldham match. The tbree-quarters will not be selected until just before the I match,but Attewell, another native of Warrington, is I one of the five from which the four men will be finally I chosen. Two other Warrington natives—Gallop and W. Ashton, the brother of the Warrington secretary, have , also been chosen, so there may be four Warrington men in the Leigh team. Tobin, a Leigh Irishman, who has been showing fine form with the Leigh Rangers, is to be tried for the first time.

The Leigh team is :—E. Clarkson (captain), back ; S. .Thlinson, H. Bennett, W. Leah, and W. Attewell, or R. H. Neville, three-gnarter backs ; J. A. Lavery and IT. Woodward, half hacks ; P. O'Neill, A. Lee, J. Tobin, W. Smith, R. Gallop, and W. Ashton,forwards.

On Saturday Warrington will journey to Widnes fr the return game, and a stern struggle may be expected, as the Widnesians are an improving side. The visiting team will be chosen after the Christmas Day match.

The " A " team to play Widnes " A" at Wilderspool will be : Mclntyre, full back; Bradshaw, Cartwright, Jordan and Lawlor, three-quarters; Lloyd and Damn- tith, half backs; Harmer, Belton, Naylor, Hewitt, Gibbs and Harrison, forwards.

The postponed home match with St. Helens has been arranged to take place on Easter Monday, April 12th. There was considerable satisfaction for supporters of the Warrington team in Saturday's match with Broughton Rangers. A peculiar pleasure is taken at Wilderspool in the downfall of certain teams, and the Rangers are among the mighty whom Warrington love to humble. Like Salford and Swinton they are Manchester's pets, and it does them good occasionally to get a whipping. Too much adulation is not good for any team.

I

ih

. e.d

I

-- As a matter of fact Broughton Rangers have fallen somewhat from their high estate. They used to be regularly in the running for the League championship but the position proved too exacting to maintain, and I do net suppose it will harm the Manchester men much to occupy a humbler sphere. Men like R. Wilson and the james's have proved bard to replace and there is no doubt that the calibre of the Rangers back division to-day is weaker than it was a few years ago by a good deal. The forwards are a capital lot, whose only fault seems to be an inclination to rough it more than is necessary. They were the best part of the team at Warrington on Saturday and had rather the better of the duel with the '.T.'arrington six.

As I anticipated, Wigan just managed to secure the Lancashire Cup after a grim struggle with Oldham,wbo lost their second final in succession at Wheater's Field. The margin was only a single point after a ding dong game, and Wigan owe their victory largely to the opportunism of Johnny Thomas and L. B. Todd. The gate was £6OO, an eloquent testimony to the increasing success of the competition. Both County Cup holders have now been beaten, for Hunslet had to bow the knee to Halifax.

MR. LOUIS HENSHALL BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

WARRINGTON AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,

I must thank many of my members for kind wishes for a Happy Christmas. Needless to say that I trust all of you have had a very happy time. I expect most of you have had fine fun with Santa Claus and stockings. Do you know, I really think I shall try my fortune this year and hang up a very big stocking and see if I can't get something. Do you see any reason why "grown-ups" should be passed over?

I think I mentioned last week that a sackful of clothing was on the way for my poor. Well, it has come to hand, and I cannot thank my kind members sufficiently. I will just mention one family to whom some of these garments will be sent. Some little time since a father and mother with one little child were getting on fairly well, living in a small room. The father gained a living by selling ice-cream. Trade was bad, and consequently he took to selling hot chestnuts instead. This went on for a. little time,, but he couldn't earn sufficient to pay his rent. The result was he was forced to sell his barrow to pay the rent, and the landlord turned them out into the street without any furniture. By a little help they now live in a tiny room, with one chair, two or three plates, and a straw mattress on the floor, and this is ad. No fire! —no light I—and no food Is it to be wondered that these poor people were delighted with a few articles of clothing. This week the man has obtained a little work. This is the kind of poverty that I am trying to4k help, with the assistance of my members. With Love, UNCLE NED. Welcome to S. E. Kerr and Minnie Lewis. —+--

THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THE CHRISTMAS OF A POOR CHILD AND THAT OF A RICH CHILD.

1. The difference is very great. A rich child looks forward eagerly to Christmas because it knows, besides having its stocking crammed with good things, there will be presents of all descriptions awaiting it on the breakfast table (or if the postman is late, when he arrives). Besides giving parties they will be going out to them, and they will be wearing pretty and costly dresses. 2. Poor children don't know what it is to have presents at Christmas, and many do not even have a Christmas dinner. Some can't afford it, while others have drunken parents who spend the money. They often have to go out singing carols to earn money with which to buy food. They have no party or Christmas Tree, and very often no fire or warm clothing. In some poor homes, although all luxury is lacking, there is comfort and happiness at Christmas.

They took the toys off the bit of holly and were playing with them and enjoying the sweets when a knock came to the door, and a man came in with a parcel containing many nice things—things that t.r.v they were wanting too, such as afrock for eatit-Abfg: and toys and sweets. The visitor proved to be the man who had given the shilling, and also their father, who wanted to surprise them. When the children went to bed, which was a very poor'-a, one, they all declared that that day had been _ the happiest Christmas Day they had ever ,V-' - known, although they lived in one room and r were nearly the poorest in that part of the town, at least.

A CHRISTMAS STORY.

Once upon a time there was a fairy whose name was Beautiful. It was Christmas Eve, and the children were gathered round the gay shop windows to see what they would like, when one said, "I should like a doll;" another said, " I should like a horse." Then the fairy who was listening said, "You shall have just what you. want." "Oh," said one, "we have no money to buy them." "You shall have them," said the kind fairy. "So go to bed like good little children." When they woke in the morning they found just what they had wanted. Then they jumped and clapped their hands and began to be happy. "Do you know why we ought to be happy?" "Yes, because our Lord Jesus Christ was born on Christmas Day." Then the elderly girl began to weep. "Don't cry," said the other, " what is the matter." " I was only thinking of our dear Mamma who told us all about it." Then they went out to see if they could find the kind fairy, but they could not. " I should think she has gone to see sonic more little girls like ourselves," they said. This was the best Christmas they had ever had since their dear parents were dead.

K. WILSON.

STERLING HOME TREATMENT FOR REDUCING WEIGHT.

" Secret " remedies, aided by fasting and exhaust- ing gymnastics, have had their day as " cures " for over-fatness. They have been tried and found want- ing. Here, at last, we have a harmless remedy open to the world ; the prescription is published broadcast. You can mix it yourself, or your chemist will do so. The following are the ingredients with quantities required , One ounce of Marmola, one ounce of fluid extract of Glycyrrhiza 8.P., one ounce of pure Glycerine 8.P., and 3} oz. of Peppermint water. I Take two teaspoonfuls of this pleasant mixture after each meal and at bedtime. In a very short while you will be astonished at the wonderful decrease of weight brought about by this sterling home treatment, more specially as you are not required to do any fasting 'Cr exercising, nor to depart in any way from your usual habits. The mixture is most beneficial to the system generally, the blood being purified and the cit culation improved; lastly, there will be no wrinkles I left to suggest a too rapid falling away of flesh.

HINTS FOR THE HOME.

Cut cucumber in thin slices, fry it, and BM to the soup in the tureen.

New bread may be cut quite evenly if the knife is very slightly heated.

Pickles should never be kept in glazed ware, as the vinegar forms a poisonous com- pound with the glazing.

Melted alum can be used for uniting metal to glass. Melt it in an iron spoon over the fire.

A few drops of glycerine in cold water will stop hiccoughs. For an infant, one or two drops of it in half a teaspoonful of water is sufficient.

When cooking a leg of mutton the intro- duction of a clove of garlic will be found a great improvement by those who like savoury dishes.

A good way to clean a copper kettle is to fill it with hot water and rub the surface with milk that has turned sour for some time. Then polish with a leather.

Soup and stock should be boiled up every day in summer and put in clean dry basins. If this precaution be taken, it will keep sweet for several days.

Spots of acid on clothing may be removed, and the colour restored by rubbing with chloroform. Ink stains on books may be re- moved without injury to the print. by apply- ing oxalic acid. Remove grass stains by rub- bing well in alcohol, milk, or molasses.

Before using a new saucepan always fill it with water, add a lump of soda, and some potato peelings, and let all stew for some hours; then wash out thoroughly, and all danger of poisoning from the tinned lining will be gone.

Lemons can be preserved for a long time if kept on a saucer with an inverted glass bowl or wide tumbler as a covering. By this method they are not excluded from the light, but they remain practically air-proof.

Christmas had arrived at last. It really was the happiest time of all the year. The children had been preparing for weeks and deciding what to give each little sister, brother, cousin, or friend. They had bought the things for mother and father, but had to wait and see what mother and father would give them at break- fast on Christmas Morning. All the presents from Santa Claus were in stockings, but the other preAnts were either on the Christmas Tree or on their chair or plate at breakfast. The house was decorated from top to bottom. It was bed-time on Christmas Eve, and six children had gone to bed, after hanging stockings up and hoping that Santa Claus wouldn't forget them when he came down the chimney, or some other awkward place. He did not forget them, certainly, for when the children opened their stockings they found just what they wanted. The girls had a doll on the top, and the boys had a box that would not go in. In this was a clockwork train and lines. Then farther down there were different things in the heeirthere was an orange, then nuts in the toe. When they got downstairs there, on their plates, were just the things they wanted. Then dinner came, with the turkey and plum pudding. Then the fun commenced. The children were surprised by seeing long strings going in and out every- where. They had to follow these, perhaps from the mistletoe in the hall up to the bed-rooms and then down to the cellar, and after you have followed the string there it will very likely end behind the piano or pictures with a cracker at the end of it, or some other toy. Then the crackers are pulled, and very likely frighten some of the children. Then, after tea, all the guests go into the drawing-room and see the Christmas Tree laden with candles, presents, crackers, sweets, etc. Then the little ones have hide-and-seek, blind-man's-buff, and other games, which make them hot and excited, and are glad to sit still and listen to tales about the house. Then, after a few more games, they are put to bed, tired and sleepy. Then the older ones will dance, or ibthere is a ghost try and find it; or somebody will suggest masks, and the ladies will dress up, and then the fun of telling a lady who is masked how funny some- body is, and it turns out to be the lady who is being talked about. The tree is taken down and all the presents are. taken away, and for once that day it is quiet. S. E. KERR.

It was Christmas Eve in the afternoon, about 4.30, in the busy town of Sheffield two children ~Were earnestly looking into the shop windows land saying what they would each like, when an old gentleman came up to them and said, "110 w is it you are not out with your father and mother?" The little boy answered, "Because father is a drunkard and mother would gladly have come with us only she has no money to spend, and it makes her cry to see other people buylng_thituzs for their children and her not being able to." The old man, with tears in his eyes, took the two children by the hand and led them into a clothing shop, where he purchased for them numerous articles of clothing which • the children were very thankful for. Then he bought them a numb& of toys and plenty of good food to eat so that they would be able to enjoy Christmas Day. Just before the little girl and boy left the old man they kissed him several times. Then they wandered home to their poor mother, who was so thankful that she wept for joy. Just before they sat down to their t. a they all knelt down to thank God in preparing them such a lovely meal. After this day their . father became -a"tegtotaler, and was often st.en with his wife and neatly-dressed children in the little church close by, singing and praising God for delivering him from the horrible drink. MOTOR CAT AND CYCLE REOLTLATIONS.

Each one must be registered with the council of a county or county borough, the fee being .C 1 bored motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A register a number is assigned to each, which it Is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences. _ . .

---YahTghest speed at which a car may he driven on the public highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drivo a car in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerovs to the public, hiving regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to be presence of the motor car on the road, the river must supply his name and address, and the twner and number of the car, under heavy penal- ode The driver must produceh is licence at the sequest of a police constable.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.'

ALLOYS OF GOLD AND IRON.

Iron and gold R hen melted together may be mixed in various proportions, and hard. ened or crystallised. Alloys containing 10 per cent, of gold are harder than pure iron, but as the proportion of gold increases above that amount, the alloy becomes softer. Silver and iron do not form alloys.

The Examiner, Thursday, December 24, 1908,

===== 0002601_19081224_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
RED LIGHT VISIBLE FAR.

Red lights have been proved to be visitira to a greater distance than green. In recent tests on a clear, dark night, a red light of one candle power was clearly discernible at one mile, one of three candle power at two miles, ten candle power, through a binocular, at four miles, and thirty-three candle power at five miles. On an exceptionally clear night e white light of 3-2 candle power could be dig. tinguished at three miles and 17-2 candle power at five miles.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STORM-NOSE AT SEA.

The picturesque name of Storm-none (Gewitternase) is given in Germany to the wave of high barometric pressure which often precedes a storm or a heavy squall. The baro- meter rises suddenly, and then falls more gradually. It is believed that this phenome- non is responsible for sudden changes in the level of the sea. Observations on the seas surrounding Denmark have led to the conclu- sion that the change of level thus produced sometimes amounts to no lees than 31t.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY THE SEA IS BLUE.

The sea is blue because the water reflects the blue rays of light, but shallow seas are green because the blue light is mixed with the yellow reflections from sand and stones at the bottom. Green is a mixture of blue and yellow. In this green light of shallow water all seaweeds grow, and, for want of the red rays, they have golden and tawny leaves. Green and red seawoecls are the exception, and blue scaweeds are as rare as blue tree loaves. At this rate, land plants grown under green glass ought to turn golden brown, like seaweed. They do. Experiment has shewn that under green glass plants grow nearly As well as under clear sunlight. BLOOD TEMPERATURE OF ATHLETES. Professor Flack records some curious ob- servations on the blood temperature of run- ners. The normal blood temperature in man is about 98.11 deg. Fehr. A young man, after a run of 200 yards, sliewed a blood tempera- ture of 100•76 deg. ; another a temperature of 100.94 deg. ; a third a temperature of 102•2c1eg. after a run of half a mile. A mile run produced an internal temperature of 102.8 deg. with one athlete, and 103.6 deg. with another. After a three-mile run one .young man had a temperature of 1051eg. But this runner's normal blood temperature was 101 deg., although he was in perfect health.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
PURE HYDROGEN FOR BALLOONS.

The development of aeronautics hae given importance to the production of hydrogen, illuminating gas having gone out of fashion as completely as Montgolfier's hot air, and for the same reason—because it is too heavy. In an American method of producing hydro- gen, water gas is first made in the usual way, by passing steam over live coal. The result- ing water gas, which is a mixture of hydro- gen and carbon monoxide, is then passed over hot pulverised calcium carbide. The carbon monoxide is decomposed, the carbon remaining behind in the form of graphite and the oxygen combiniv with the carbide to form calcium carbonate, leaving the hydrogen almost pure (97 per cent.). It is claimed that hydrogen can be produced very cheaply by this process.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
UPPER-AIR TEMPERATURE.

Balloonists and mountain-climbers have long known that the temperature of the air falls as the altitude increases. It has recently been discovered that this decrease in tem- perature has its limits. " Sounding" bal- loons, freighted with automatic recording in- struments, have been sent to heights far ex- ceeding those which any balloonist can hope to reach. The records obtained chew that at a height of about eight miles the thermometer ceases to fall, and may even rise. The dis- tinguished French meteorologist Tisserano do I3ort claims also to have discovered that at a certain level the air above the poles is warmer than that above the equator, an anomaly wlAieli mo.t be more fully demon- stroted than is now possible before it can be accepted.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
A WANDERING PLANT.

Curious among vegetabl9 growths and one which is seldom seen of men is the rootless cactus of the Californian desert. This plant, a round, compact growth, rolls. about the level floor of the desert for some eight or nine months of the year, tossed hither and thither by the winds which blow with fierceness over all of California's sand plateaux, during those months. At the coming of the rains, or rather the cloudbursts, which sweep the desert in its springtime, this cactus takes root wherever it happens to have been dropped by the lass v‘ind of which it was the plaything, and im- mediately begins to put out all around it small shoots, which, in turn, become cacti, exactly like the parent plant. The roots do not penetrate the soil deeply, but spread often over a circle whose radius is not less than 10ft. These plants get every bit of mois- ture and plant food to be had in the territory they cover.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COLOURS OF THE CLOUDS.

Clouds are but masses of vapour, and their colour is white by reflected light as we usu- ally see them, and of various colours as we see them by transmitted ligkt. Light from the setting sun passing through clouds will be to a greater or less extent absorbed, and this absorption, says the Optical Journal, will be regular or chromatic in proportion to the amount of watery vapour in the lower layers at the atmosphere. Where there is very little watery vapour to cut off the light the clouds will shew some shade from white to black ac- cording to the thickness of the condensed vapour which forms the cloud, but where the invisible watery vapour in suspension between the eve of the observer and the clouds is con- siderable, then the clouds will seem to be coloured. This is due to the fact that this watery vapour has the power of absorbing blue, and hence the colour of the cloud as it really is does not come to us but only that part of its colour which is left when the blue has been removed, and this will be some shade of red. The clouds themselves are mist and are white; their brilliant colour is but a delusion.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
MARVELS OF SMELL.

How infinitely minute must I;(7—th. € particles that emanate from the object which the dog is tracking! Yet, according to an eminent scientist of the Government, the matter is ex- tremely divisible. The tenth part of a grain of musk will con- tinue for years to Jill a room with its odori- ferous particles, and at the end of that time will not be appreciably diminished in weight by the finest balance. A cubic inch of air rising from the flame of a Bunsen burner has been found to contain no fewer than 489,000,000 dust particles. A drop of blood which might be suspt lad from the point of a needle contains about- 1,000,000 of red flattenc4 corpuscles. Still, though mat- ter is so marvellously divisible, the olfactory nerves are

===== 0002601_19081224_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
INFINITELY MORE SENSITIVE.

Much has yet to be investigated with regard to the differentiation of . the points in these nerves so that they may discriminate with such apparently miraculous accuracy; vet even the results in the scent of dogs shew how wonderfully fine is their discriminating power. . . . .

Our sense of smell, unless in the trained chemist, is not even so acute as that of the semi-savage. The aborigines of Peru can, in the darkest night and in the thickest woods, distinguish, respectively, a white man, a negro, and 9ne of their own race by the smell. Much have we gained by civilisation, but not without some loss to our bodily energies and senses. Man's recuperative power after an injury is, in the opinion of the highest authorities, in the inverse ratio to his social advancement. Similarly he seems to become less acute awl delicate in the sense of smell as he fares better and lives more comfortably.

Warrington Avenged on the Rangers.

The annual visit of 13roughton Rangers to Warrington provided a fine struggle between two good teams on Saturday. Warrington lost at Broughton early in the season, but they NI are weakly represented that day. On Saturday they were at .full strength and easily out- pointed the Rangers. A drizzling rain spoiled the match in many ways, but there was an attendance of quite 4,000, and some good football was seen. Teams and officials : Warrington—Tilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Trehearne,three-quarter backs; Jenkins and Brooks, half backs ; Thomas' O'Neill, Dowell, P. Boardman, A. E. Boardman, and Cheaters, forwards. Broughton Rangers—Harris, back • Barr, Hogg, C. James, and Hardyman, three-quarter backs ; Flynn and Harry, half backs ; J. L. Clampitt, Beetham, Mottram. Gminey, Wylde, and Wileman, forwards. Referee, Mr. .1. F. May, St. Helens. Touch judges, Messrs. R. Jones' ' Widnes, and W. 0. Johnson Salford. The Rangers started in strong style, and the home backs being unsteady in fielding at the start the Warrington line was endangered. Tilley fly-kicked in front of a forward rush and missed the ball. A try , seemed certain as the visiting front rank bore down on the line, but Dowell dived for the ball and managed to get the touch-down. After this narrow escape, Warring- ton removed play to the other end, some fine play being ' witnessed. Scrummaging followed near the line, and IJenkins picking up plunged through the ruck and grounded near the posts. Fish failed at goal, but the home crowd were now on good terms with themselves. Strong forward play by the Rangers altered the aspect of affairs. Flynn passed to Barr, and the burly Wiganer only succumbed at the corner to the united assault of Tilley and Brooks. Warrington bad some axioas moments, but a penalty against Flynn gave relief. Midfield play followed, both sides made excel- lent passing runs. Hardyman carried play nearly to the home line before be was sent in touch. Warrington got possession here and a great effort by Brooks got the ball away to centre again. Here Jenkins secured and bluffing the Broughton halves passed to Brooks. The latter dashed through at top speed with Dickenson and Fish in attendance and a perfect piece of combination resulted in the wing man scoring behind the posts, and also adding a goal. Half-time : Warrington 8 points, Broughton nil. The second half was strongly contested, and the visiting front rank had an advantage iu the scrummages. Warrington dribbled well, however, and O'Neill was cheered for a fine piece of footwork which took play nearly in the line. The usual penalty followed and the Rangers got away. Hardyman ran speedily and cross- kicked in front of goal,where Hogg was perfectly placed but knocked the ball on. This was the best chance of a try that the Rangers had, Warrington did some clever running and passing, Fish making one fine effort on his own, but the slippery turf failed him after he had dodged two men. On the other side Barr struggled hard to cross, but bundled into touch. Flynn again got into trouble for " putting in " and Thomas landed a penalty goal for Warrington. Little ot note happened after- wards, except one fine run by C. James. Final : War- rington, 2 tries 113 points ; Broughton Rangers nil.

THE MERSEY RIVALS DRAW.

Widnes and Runcorn met a Widnes before 3,000 Spectators. Early on Padhury had liosorrossfol ahotß at goal, and then Ditchfield broke away with a clear field, but as he was being overhauled he kicked up the and a glorious chance VMS lost through one of the Widnes men getting off-side. When in the home "25 " Jolley initiated a passing movement, and Deakin scored a good try at the corner. Padbury failed at goal. Half-time score :—Runcern 1 try (3 points), Widnes nil. Early on in the second half the visitors' defence was troubled, and Deakin in trying to run the ball out lost possession when tackled, rand Sam Aspey scored. Jones failed to improve. Jones and G. Aspey failed at goal kicks, and the match ended in a draw of 1 try each.

ST. HELENS FINE DISPLAY AT SALFORD. Salford and St. Helens met at the Willows, Weaste, under wretched conditions. From the start the home side took up the attack, but Armstrong prove very safe, and was the means of sending play to the centre. The visitors continued to hold the advantage,and from some good forward work Prescott was presented with a very easy try, and Greenwood added the goal. Play after this point WAS fairly interesting, and Lomas with a capital place-kick, reduced the visitors' lead to three points. Still the visitors were not to be shaken off and after some splendid work by the St. Helens forwards Kelly nipped in and scored. Greenwood on this occa- sion striking the posts in his attempt to place a goal. The home side redoubled their efforts, and Lomas kicked a goal and Cook scored an unimproved try before the interval.

The second portion proved highly interesting, and when Byman was awarded a try for obstruction the home supporters were on good terms with themselves. This, however, was short lived, for in quick succession Barton was credited with a couple of tries, 'which were not improved upon. The home side after these reverses made desperate efforts to obtain the lead, and eventu- ally, following really good work by Cook, Lomas was enabled to beat Armstrong for possession and score, the same player adding the goal points, and thus placing the home side in front. Result—Salford 3 goals, 3 tries (15 points) ; St. Helens 1 goal, 4 tries (14 points).
