L.N.-W. MANAGER RETIRES. ---- His Probable Successor.

Sir Frederick Harrison has resigned the position of general manager of the London and North-Western Railway, and has accepted a seat on the board of the company. It is considered probable that the directors will , appoint Mr. Frank Ree, the present chief goods ' manager, to the position of general manager. Sir Frederick Harrison, who received his knight- hood in 1902, has been general manager of the London , and North-Western Railway since 1893, succeeding the late Sir George Findlay in that year. Mr. Frank Ree, it is interesting to note, was at one time the company's district goods manager in Liver- pool. He left that position to take up his present duties in London in 1893. Our readers will probably recollect that in April 1907, Mr. and :qrs. Frank flee visited Warrington on the occasion of the opening of the Railway Institute at Bank Quay.

===== 0002601_19081224_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
P.S.A. MEETINGS.

Warrington Society's Musical. Service:

A musical service arranged by the Warrington P.S.A Society was held in the Co-operative Hall on Sunday afternoon and attracted a large gathering. The ex-Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presided, and after making reference to the poverty and crime existing in our midst and which it was the duty of Christians to try and remedy, expressed his satisfaction that the day appeared to be coming when the churches would knit together, regardless of creed or dogma, with one grand object in view, that of taking the Gospel into every lane and alley of the town. He also spoke on eulogistic terms of the work of the Salvation Army. Miss E. Taylor sang the solos Come unto Me "and " Entreat me not to leave thee "with much taste and expression. Miss Howell, who appeared in place of Mrs. Conron, was heard to advantage in " The better land" Violin solos were admirably given by Messrs. H. EL Pearson and A. Bete& Mr. J. E. Hannan, contributed "The Star of Bethlehem "and "The sailor's grave " with good effect. Mr. Herbert Plumpton, who possesses a I very powerful bass voice, sang "The outcast " and " Behold Me standing at the door," in a manner which greatly impressed the audience. The St. Barnabas' Musical Society orchestra was in attendance and, under the conductorship of Mr. H. J. Iddon played" Largo " (flctiulel) and " Tannhauser March " (ltligner) in a manner which •evidenced good progress. Mr. Frank Hickman and Mr. Forster were the accompanists. The whole of the arrangements for the service were carried out by Mr. Starkey. The lesson was read by the President (Mr. Nelson.)

THE WYCLIFFE SOCIETY. A well-attended meeting of this Society was held on Sunday, when special Christmas hymns were sung, including " Christians awake " and "While shepherds watched their flocks by night." Mr. Farrar presided and spoke of the pleasure it. gave to young and old to look forward to the celebrating of Christ's birth. He wished all the brethren and others " A very merry and happy Christmas "in the truest sense of the term. Mr Blane gave a short address upon " Goodwill to men.'- He said that, with the Chairman, he had very pleasing' memories of the Christmas of his youth. It was a great pleasure to look forward to these happy se:sons. Mr. Warburton sang " Blest Redeemer" and " The gates of gold," and was well received. Mr. Payton pre- sided at the organ. BUCKLEY-STREET SOCIETY.

An open meeting of the Buckley-street P.S.A. Society was hold on Sunday when selections were given from the " Messiah " by the Legh-street Primitive Methodist choir. 111 r. J. Beswick presided. The soloists were Miss E. Taylor. Miss L. Plinston, Mr. W. J. Booth and Mr. E. H. Pearce. Miss Plinston sang " lie was despised "and "0 Thou that tellest " ; Miss Taylor effectively interpreted "There were shepherds" an " Glory to God " ; while Messrs Booth and Pearce were equally successful with their selections. Mr. A. Spruce conducted and Mr. A. W. Clatworthy presided at the organ.

For all Chronic Chest Complaints, take Woods' Great Peppermint Cure. is. IW. 2s. 9cl.—Advt.

[By MR. H. Winerreet] The above heading is startling, might be thought sensational, but it is awfully real. Before the follow- ing story can be published in the " Examiner" the wires will have flashed around the world the -news of one of the most startlingievents that ever thrust itself into the midst of a civilized community. All the world knows of the graft that is, in so bare-faced a fashion, doing its beat, or worst, to undermine and overthrow the democratic principles that filled the breasts of the good men and true who laid the Soundaticns of the North American Republic, and all the world knows also that to-day the greed for money-getting and the worship of self are the twin evils that are robbing that same Republic of every vestige of that nobility of life that must accompany a real and lasting civilization. Among the nations of the world the United States takes a little consolation to itself by saying, " Oh, well other nations are just as bad as we are," and there is some truth in this charge. Graft has made Russia a thoroughly rotten nation. Her war with Japan proved it. Graft caused thousands of lives to be uselessly and criminally lost in the war waged, when the writer was a boy, between England and Russia, and again in the South African war. More recent still, graft has caused in Denmark, thousands of hard-working farmers to lose their all through the dishonesty of a Minister of the Crown. " Graft "is a euphemism applied to a" higher-up' wrong-doer. "Thief, and criminal "are words we apply to the poor fellow who steals a loaf for his starving child. San Francisco, a city of between three and four hundred thousand people, was, sixty years ago, an almost unknown quantity. To reach California at that time was a task undertaken only by the venturesome, and to reach San Francisco, death by numberless routes awaited the daring pioneer. Toe call of- the wild went hand in hand with the call of gold and perseverance brought stccess. In " the days of '49 " law and order had to fight for their very existence against lawlessness of every conceivable description. The right prevailed ; the vigilance committees repro- ; seated the best elements of the lands in which they i were born and reared. The evil elements also were representative and to-day an outsider might think, i but would think wrongly' they were dominant. The - - work of the evil-doer is glaring. It strikes the public eye, but as we know the wages of sin are death. At this present moment, while I am writing, the whole of the Pacific Coast, with San Francisco as its centre, is in the wildest whirlpool of excitement it has ever known in its brief history, and graft, bribery, corruption and thievery are the roots and cause of it all. 'Yet the excited crowds of the streets are not The happy arrangement of holding the annual Volun- losing their heads. It simply means that after years of Christmas time is being continued now that the new underhanded corrupt officialism Justice, though Territorial system is in force, and there was a large blind, shall have the road cleared for action. And amid the rejoicings of the extremists, and to the regret of a great body of moderate opinion drawn from all parties, the further progress of ,the bill was abandoned. Meantime, other important measures of social reform were enabled throusth the beneficent result of the new Committee system introduced by Sir Henry Camp- bell-Bannerman to be simultaneously pass. ing into law. The Children's Bill, the Miners' Eight Hours Bill, and the Hous- ing and Town Planning Bill were thus discussed in detail, and approved by bodies of experts sitting on the Committees of the House of Commons. The long session, in which only one month of the year had not shewn a sitting of Parliament, drew towards its end -with a harvest of legislation of which any House of Commons might have been I proud; the Liberal Party, still maintain- ing its ranks unimpaired, and with the Irish and the Labour Parties working generally in conjunction with it, faced at the end a still disunited Op- position. Within that Opposition Pro- , tection was revealing itself as sweeping all opponents aside, and evidently determined to dominate the party in the future. Those few Free Traders who had still main- tained some semblance of a fight one by one either revealed themselves as quiescent or were branded for future destruction. Mr. Balfour, who maintained his Leader- ship in the House by ability of dialectic, without any stimulating programme which could excite enthusiasm in the country, shewed himself as not insensitive to this- change, and as more and more prepared to accept, in his own phraseology, the sub- stance of what was demanded by a party now preaching Protection naked and un- ashamed. It was very evident at the end that the Constitutional question of the challenge of the Commons by the Lords, and the economic question cf the challenge of the British Free Trade system by the 'l nee-Protectionists, are destined to over- '! shadow in the immediate future all other j political questions. At the dinner in his :-.. honour at the National Liberal Club, Mr. 4 Asquith said that the question of the vete ' A of the Lords would be the dominating issue ,to at the next General Election. .1 Parliamentary incidents of an unusual * kind were for the most part lacking, being I chiefly confined to the efforts of ladies call- '.*, ing themselves Suffragettes to obtain en- :14 trance to the House. Most of these were 4 organised attempts from outside to break through cordons of policemen, which re- sulted in little but unseemly scrimmages and arrests. The:. I majority of Members found little moon-.' venience in such a cholla°. Mr X OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. 449 Carol Service at Legh-street. A carol service was held on Sunday evening as Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church presided ove j by Rev. J.B. Buglass, who also gave a short address The service consisted of choruses, anthems, solos and le hymn-carols, as follows : Miss A. Bridge," There were shepherds," "To the angel," " And suddenly," which was followed by the chorus 0 Glory to God." Miss L. Snelling rendered "God who math:at earth and heaven." - Mr. J. Willett gave" Star of Bethlehem," the choir also rendered "And the glory," " Lift up your heads "and the anthem "The Lord gave the word" (Turner). A number of hymm-carols were also sung by the choir and congregation and the service proved most enjoy- able. Mr. A. Spruce conducted and Mr. A. W. Clat- worthy presided at the organ with his usual_ability. The Vision of Peace. Preaching at Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday morning, the Rey. R. E. Letheren made refer- ence to the Peace question. Be took for his text "In the last days it shall come to pass " (Micah 4-1.) In the course of a powerful sermon, Mr. Letheren said that soi,lety bad now discontinued the COMNIG es .i. mua..) of settling individual differences yet retained it for the settlement of differences between communities. But the sense of justice was growing which would dis- credit that foolish and brutal practice in the one case as It had done in the other. Moreover it must be remembered that at a comparatively recent time in the history of mankind, battle was regarded by men of our own race as a religious rite. In the course of the middle ages the undertaking of the Church to establish the Truce of God was considered quite as chimerical as would be a proposal for universal disarmament in our own time. Nevertheless,it was established, and brought peace to a vast region which had therotofore been the scene of endless rapine and murder. He (the preacher) was not particularly fond of figures, but ho could not refrain from reminding them of how marvellously this feature of Micah's vision of peace had been justified by fact in our, own days. In the 9 years 1841-59 six cases of arbitration were established among nations; 1891-1900, 62 ; and in the three years 1901-3, 63. What- ever else war might be, few would deny that it was waste. It was a waste of material. Did any sane man consider that we had got anything like money's worth for the more than 230 millions that the war in South Africa cost us ? When they had put aside the con- tractors, the mine-owners and the officers who were pen- sioned, who was there who was a penny the better off . for it ? When they considered the desperate needs of hich one our country that could not be met, And our eternal want w or pence, who would not agree that war expenditure In the ss,- - was a wicked waste? It was a waste of mind. Some of the cleverest inventors, the cleverest thinkers, the most capable organizers gave all their time to perfect engines and methods of destruction. " No !" said some " not for destruction, but defence." What did they defend? It was not the weapons of the Germans, French, or Americans we need fear. It was their superior educational facilities. Our army did not defend our trade. It, was not a foreign foe that slew every year 128,000 of the people within the borders of the United Kingdom. It was drink. It was no foreign foe that had depopu- lated our countryside and left one on the land for five that formerly found is livelihood there. It was not the sword or spear of the foreigner that swelled the ranks of the unemployed. War failed to defend the very interests it proposed to make it its business to preserve. Then put up the sword. The voice of Christ once more speaks in the presence of the cannon's roar. Oh ! men and brothers let that voice be heard. War fails, try peace ; put up that useless sword." A representative of one of our daily papers was once interviewing a notable advocate of peace and he noticed over the fireplace a beautiful sword handle and asked him the purpose of such a warlike weapon in the abode of peace. "Ah, yes" was the reply "that's a sword from the Crimea. I could not turn it into a plough share so I turned it into a poker," he said as he gently stirred the fire. "If" (continued the speaker) "we cannot at once turn our swords into plough shares let us turn them lute pokers. We could stir into a flame the dying. sparks that shall illuminate the firesides of friendship among neighbouring nations. We would let some fresh air into our dull embers heaped upon the hearths of homesteads." It was better to help to build up the body than to batter it to fragments. The manifold needs of men in mind, soul, body. and estate were enough to demand all our energies and to these we must conse- crate our richest resources whether of mind or material.

LIVERPOOL JUNIOR CUP. THIRD ROUND.

RvLArDS'V. BIAINDELLSANDS.—ConSiderabIO interest was centred in this match which was played on Itylands Bros.' ground under depressing conditions on Saturday. The wretched state of the ground favoured the visitors, who were of finer physique than the wiremen. Both teams opened equal, each end being visited in turn. Eventually Luecock got the home team going, but was checked before he was dangerous. Play was transferred , and Martin came in useful at a critical juncture. newton was resourceful at back and played a consist- ently good game. When Rylands were again attacking , there was an infringement in the visitors' penalty area. ' Warren took the penalty kick, but the visiting custodian received the ball in his hands and cleared easily. The , home forwards lacked finish, and many shots were astray ' through injudicious kicking. Blundollsands attacked and forced several corners, but Booth effected easy clearances. One shot•went over the bar. There was no score at the interval. In the second bait Spencer scored twice for the home team, the final result being Rylands', two goals ; Elundellsands, nil. For Children's tackingeough at night, Wood's Great Peppermint Cure. is. 13. 2s. 9d.—Advt.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. -- " Cinderella." Pantomime is one of the time-honoured amusements of this festive season —in fact Christmas without a pantomime would be sadly incomplete. Those desirous of enjoying this seasonable form of entertainment should see Messrs. Pardoes' and Miss Lottie Holland's production of " Cinderella" which entered upon a fortnight's run at the FLoyal Court Theatre on Monday evening. It was very very well received by an appre- ciative audience and good business h ould be the rule during the holidays. The story of Cinderella and the magic glass slipper, which never loses its charm for the rising generation, forms the groundwork of a medley of mirth, music and frivolity and the show is fall of life and briskness of action from beginning to end. Miss Lottie Holland, as Prince Charming, makes a dashing principal boy and finds great favour with several pleasing vocal numbers. Miss Nellie Rose is an attractive Cinderella who :passes her spare moments in singing and dancing, in which she is well accomplished. Miss Cissie Loveday (late of Mr. George Edwardes' Co.) makes the most of the part of the fairy godmother and sings "Starlight " with conspicuous success. Miss Lizzie Round and Miss Beattie Howard as Dandini and Princess Rosala respectively, are a gay couple who achieve considerable popularity. Mr. Tom Griffitt (late of the Carl Rosa Opera Co.) does admirably as Father Time, his robust voice finding i full scope n the always welcome songs " Revenge ' and " The Storm Fiend." The humorous element is, of course, well to the fore. Chief among the merry- makers is Mr. George Hyam as Scoot, the man in possession, and his song in which he declares his willingness to live on a desert island or beard ferocious lions rather than encounter suffragettes is one of the hits of the evening. The fun of the thing is heightened when a body of suffragettes appear on the scene and harangue an imaginary audi- ence on votes for women, ending in giving vent to their feelings in pugilistic fashion to the discomfiture of the mere man who happens to cross their path. All Grady and Mike Hastings are also very laughable as Blobbs and Blabbs, " pattering" and dancing being their specialities. The impersonations of the ugly sisters by the Bros. Dench are a triumph in " make-up " and Mr. T.Dorma cuts a ludicrious figure as the impecunious Baron. The scenic setting of the pantomime is elaborate and the magnificent coach, resplendent with electric lights, in which Cinderella goes to the ball excites much admiration. There are several excellent special turns. The marvellous Pardoes delight the audience with a wonderful arrobatic display and Zenieth,an animal impersonator, as Jacko, the mischievous monkey, displays extra- ordinary agility. Miss Lottie Holland assisted by six ladies, gives the attractive scena " Ayesha, my sweet Egyptian " which nightly meets with great approval. The Edingale Trio of Continental speciality dancers are experts in their particular line of business and add not a little to the success of the production. There will be special afternoon performances of " Cinderalla " on Boxing Day, Friday, January Ist and Saturday, January 2nd. FUTURE ARRA.NGEMENTS. The production of " Cinderella " will be the attrac- tion during the whole of next week. An important and expensive engagement of The Moody-Manners Grand Opera Company will be commenced on January 11th for one week only, when a programme of favourite operas will be submitted. The manage- 'meat have adopted the unique idea of allowing their patrons to vote for the operas which they wish to see performed. Voting papers have been distributed, containing the titles of fourteen operas, and the pro- gramme for the week will be arranged according to the result of this test. Followini, in quick succession the theatre will be visited by : "'Raffles " (the amateur cracksman), " Peter's mother," a grand and magnificent pantomime of " Dick Whittington," "The " The Christian," "Are you a Mason." F. R. Benson's Company, several favourite dramas, and also "The Lady of Ostend " by the Warrington Amateur Dramatic Society.

PRESENTATION TO MR. G. CARRUTHERS.

Mr. George Carruthers, second master at the Boteler Grammar School, who has just been appointed Mathe- matics Master at the Municipal Secondary School, Whitworth-street, Manchester, was on Saturday pre- sented by the boys of the II rammar School with a parting gift in the form of a handsome writing desk and a solid silver inkstand. The proceedings took place at the Heathside School in the presence of a large gathering of boys and " old boys. Mr. 13. Brown pre- sided and the presentation was made by Mr. J. Sterling, the head boy, both expressing regret at Mr. Carruthers' departure and speaking most warmly of his kindness to those under his charge. Mr. CaßnuntEtts who was received with deafening cheers on rising to retwn thanks, said he had been at the Grammar Reboot nea-lir twelve years, he had made lots of friends in Warrington, and he could say with a perfectly clear conscience that during the whole of that time he had scarcely met one single boy that he did not like. (Applause.) He was going to a better post in some respects, not so much on account of the actual work, or even the salary, as on acoount of the fact that the school was under a more progressive education authority in Manchester. He purposed for the present to reside among his friends in Warrington. He had been in the teaching profession twenty years. The last place he was at was a boarding school, and he bad to leave because he got married. (Laughter.) He was content to do that, but he regretted having to sever his connec- tion with the Warrington Grammar School boys, who were so well disposed towards him—(applause) —and on behalf of whom the whole of his work had heed a work of complete pleasure. Schoolmasters, he knew, were eminently a nervous, irritable race. (Laughter, and hear, hear.) He did not quite realise the reason of that. He knew a master in Warrington Grammar School—not himself—so full of energy that he did not sleep at nights. (Laughter.) Being what they were they were occasionally irritable and they sometimes said things they would not say in their sober senses, and did things they would not do if they thought a little more. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) The true schoolmaster, be thought, was the man who endeavoured to do his duty to the best of his ability, and who was willing to bear with and try to understand, the child who might have caused him trouble. There were many boys with whom he had quarrelled over their work, but towards them be had always had the most pleasant feeling afterwards, and wawa ways prepared to do anything he possibly could for them. (Applause.) For himself he had been twelve years in the school and he wusted them to consider him an old Botelarian—(load cheers)—and to treat him exactly as they would treat every Old Boy. If he could do any of them a favour in after life he would he most happy to do it, and he should expect in return that everybody there would be ready to give him a helping hand in the world. It did not matter how well-placed one might be there were often occasions when a little help could do a wonderful amount of good. His last I word was : "Just treat me as an old Grammar School boy for the future." (Cheers). A vote of thanks was passed to the Rev. W. Bracecemp for the use of the room. Three ringing cheers for Mr. Carruthers were followed by three " for Sterling," and the singing of "For he's a jolly good fellow."

SHOCKING NEGLECT CASE.

Children in a Cupboard.

Martha Jane Littlewood, 67, Priestley-street, was charged at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, with neglecting her five children ir, a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to health. She pleaded guilty. Mr. Browne prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., and stated that defendant was a widow, and the mother of seven children, and had lived for 6 years at 69, Priestley-street. The house was kept going by three of the children whose combined wages amounsed to 28s. per week.—Dafenciant was addicted to drink, and instea d of providing the ordinary comforts for her children, a large portion of the money was spent in drink. The children were poorly clad and dirty, whilst the sleep - jug-rooms were in a filthy condition. Dr. Peacock stated that he went to the house on December 19, find then saw three children ranging from 12 to 6 years of age. They were very dirty and verminous. The only food in the house was a portion of a 21b. loaf. Inspector Cos was then called, and stated that he had had the defendant under his observation for 13 months. The children were in a deplorable condition. They.had only a dirty filthy mattress and a few filthy rags for a bed, which was not fit for animals to sleep upon. He had warned defendant, but for the last two months she had been as bad as ever. The poor children were clothed only in dirty rags, and he had to find clothing for them in order to take them to the Workhouse. On one occa- sion he visited the house and defendant told him the children were at school. 'He searched the house and found them in a small cupboard underneath the, stairs. The poor children were frightened out of their witts Defendant was always sodden with drink when he saw her. Robert Littlewood, one of defendant's sons, said he gave his mother 12s. out of ISs. per week. He had to buy his own clothes. His mother spent her money on drink. Defendant was sentenced to three months' hard labour.

COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. WE CORDIALLY WISH ALL OUR READER S A MERRY CHRISTMAS AND A HAPPY NEW YEAR.

Local Curacy. At the Palace, Liverpool, on Tuesday, the Bishop licensed the Rev. Jas. Harold Garnett, M.A., to a curacy at St. Peter's, Warrington. Motor Car on Fire. A motor car on its way from Manchester caught fire while some repairs were being made to it on tlie road near Holy Hedge Farm, Action Grange, on Sunday and soon became a total wreck. It had been occupied by two gentlemen but fortunate they escaped uninjured. Bewsey.road Wesley Guild. The members of this guild have again set a worthy example this Christmas by giving the proceeds of their carol-singing to a treat for poor children. Last year over £ll was raised in this manner, and 800 children were entertained to a good meal. The weekly meeting of the Guild was discontinued on Monday,and between twenty and thirty members visited the members of the congregation at their homes and rendered excellent music. Warrington Women's Circle. Mrs. James Smethurst presided at the Circle on Sunday. The Service of song " Those Merry Bells " was given by the girls' choir. Mrs. Peart's solos. " The (Alf t ' and " The Gentle Shepherd " met with hearty applause. Duets by Misses Bather and Burney were also appreciated. On Monday evening the pro- gramme was repeated at Bank Quay Mission, with the addition of a solo by Miss L. Plinston. The reading was given by the Rev. R. E. Letheren. Magistrate and Music Licences. Applications were made at the Borough Police Court, on Monday, by Robert Perdae, Higher Angel Hotel ; Agnes Potter, King's Arms ; George East- wood ; Crown and Sceptre ; and John McClinton , Royal Oak, for 11 days' music licences, to cover the Christmas and New Year holidays. Asked for his view the Chief Constable said that all lie could say was that the music would increase the takings of the lioencees. There had nit been any campla.ints in previous years, and he could not say that he had any objection to the application.—Mr. W. Roberts said his objection was to the large number of young people who were attracted to the public-houses during holi- day times, and it did not tend to promote sobriety.— Mr. Perdue :—I object to that remark. We do not encourage young people to come to our houses.—Mr. Roberts: I did not say you did.—Mr. Perdue : Welt, you had no right to say that. —The licences were granted. Representatives of the Boxing Club at Orford Barracks was well to the fore in a contest at Preston Barracks on Friday night. In the 9-stone contest Private Ainsworth, Lancashire Fusiliers, beat Private Clarks, Bth King's ; Private Watts, L.N. Lancs.' beat Private Malone, Bth King's; Private Collins, South Lanes., beat Private Wharton, Lancs. Fusiliers ; and Private Finch, East Lancs., beat Private Lloyd, L.N. Lancs. In the semi-final Watts beat Ainsworth, aad Collins defeated Finch. In the find Collins, the Warrington representative,was proclaimed the winner. BRASS AND COPPER HEARTH. SUITES, See window display for new and artistic patterns.—J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Rev. F. Vaughan Receives a Call The congregation of Ernmannuel Church were much gratified on Sunday last by an announcement made at the close of the evening service by the incumbent the Rey. Frank Vaughan. Amongst other things Mr. Vaughan referred to the fact that two years ago, he had received a unanimous invitation to become the I minister of an important London Church, which at that time he had felt it was not the will of God that he should accept. As many of the people knew that within the last few weeks he had had other shrill% r calls and possibly they would be wondering what would be the outcome of them, he made no apology for announcing his decision at a public service as by that way he would reach a larger number of his congregation than he would at a business meeting. He wanted to let them know that after carefully and prayerfully considering the matter be had come to the conclusion that his work lay here at Emmanuel Church. He thanked the people for their interest and co-opera- tion in the past, and he was anxious to win and hold the confidence and affection of his congregation. He wanted to be their " brother in Christ" to share their troubles and take part in their joys. He hoped they would gather closer together in the future and then if their church was not a full one (and he would like to see it filled) it might be one the atmosphere of which made them nobler and freer and warm in the love of God. LINSEED COMPOUND' (Aniseed, Senega, Squill itc.), for Coughs. Of Chemists only.—Advt. Lamp Breakers Punished. Two youths, William Archer, 33, Hoyle-street, and Stanley Gardener, 14, Catherine-street, were charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, with break- ing three panes of glass in a gas lamp in Lodge-lane, the property of the Warrington 'Corporation. Gardner pleaded not guilty and Archer guilty. —The deputy town clerk (Mr. A. T. Halloway) prosecuted on behalf of the Corporation, and stated that on December 14th, Dr. Markel's chauffeur, was cleaning a carriage in the yard when his attention was drawn to the breaking of glass. He went outside and he there saw the two defendants shooting at the gas lamp with catapalta. He gave information to the police, and Mr. Hallos:my ' asked the magistrates if the-6ase was proved to impose a severe penalty. —Frederick Charles Towinby was called and said he saw the defendants shooting at the lamp with catapults.—Police-Constable Cooper stated that he received information by telephone and proceeded to the spot and took the names and addresses of the two defendants.—Mr. Howard, assistant secre- tary of the gas department, said it would coat 43. 7d. ,to replace the glass and mantle. It cast 000 per year to replace breakages of this kind. Since September they had. hid 603 panes of glass broken, iii this manner, and warnings had been issued —The chairman (Mr. Roberts) said the amount of the damage would have to be paid by the ratepayers of the town and he was going to make an example of the defend- ants. Archer who pleaded guilty, would he fined 103. 6:1., and Gardener, who pleaded not guilty, would be fined 14s. 61, or 7 days' each in default. If the defendants came before the magistrates again, they would be more severely dealt with. COAGGLINE,' ' KTANX' 'MASI-UNE.' Cements for mending all things.—Advt.

P OSTAL AND SAVINLS BANK REGULATIONS --a— For further particulars see " Post Office Guide," at any Post Office. POSTAGE STAMPS. Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny . stamps are now on sale at each post office. Price 2s. INLAND CORRESPONDENCE. The term " Inland Correspondence" means cor- respondence posted within the United Kingdom, including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "Inland Correspondence " is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (b) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size. LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... ld. For every additionai 2 0z5.... 3d. A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage; a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one foot in depth. POST-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be made of ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 53 inches in length by 33 incises in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and I-inch wide) bearing the address may now be affixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY PACKET POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 20z. in weight. Printed matter, &c., not in the nature of a letter, may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether ' ported singly or with others is J. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office are treated in the post like any other printed matter. PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: For aparcel ... not exceeding 1 lb. 0 3 f exceeding 1 lb. f f 2 lb. 0 4 f f t 2 lb. 3 lb. 0 5 „ 3 lb. 5 lb. 0 6 If 5 lb. ft' 7 lb. 0 7 „ 7 lb. ft 8 lb. 0 8 „ 8 lb. ft 9 lb. 0 9 „ 9 lb. PI 10 lb. 010 9, 10 lb. " 11. lb. 011 No parcel may exceed 111 b. in weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel is : Greatest length... ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and firth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3fh ein. in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. Gin, round its thickest part ; a short parcel may be thicker ; thus, if it measure no more than Mt. in length, it may measure as much as Mt. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel " Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postage are in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions similer to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a Id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 2d. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 4d.; £2O, 15. ; £3O, Bd. ; £4O, 10d. By paying an additional 2d7and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND CoLoNIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order may be issued for places abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £l,3d. ; £2, 6d. ; and above that, 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A fee of 6d. in addition to the ordicary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POSTAL ORDERS. These are obtainable at any money order office in the United Kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed, they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by 6d., from 6d. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are :-3(1. up to 2s. 6d. ; Id. up to 155.; 1)d. les ta 91s. Before nartine with e peelel esder, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter should be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General will entertain no further claim after an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd stuns. TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,almost entirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the Uhited Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is is minimum amount of 6d., which covers 12 words, and an additional Id. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, but if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, but sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five 'figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated on payment of half the original charge, which extra payment is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is Lila. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must bo written on special forms and are accepted in the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANE. These are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £3O, no) the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot he withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is Is., rind this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in which the ernount of each deposit - • should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over. a special acknowledgment is sent from the head London office. Interest is allowed at the rate of 2S per cent. Any amount tip to and including £1 may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Hank Department for purposes of checking nneadding interest. The withdrawal of money can bee xpedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegra ms. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK LINE CHARGES. PARLIAMENTARY DIVISIONS. Local Members' Records. The members of tho House of Commons finished the protracted session of 1908 on Monday, when Parliament was formally prorogued. Since the house sat on January 29th there have been no fewer than 463 divisions. The autumn session has been unusually prolific in this respect, for since October the house has divided on 223 occasions. The total number of divisions for the year, though large, is by no means a record ; in fact, last year the totaLexceeded the present figures by three, and a long way has to be climbed to reach the dizzy heights of 1902, when the legislators tramped through the division lobbies on 618 occasions. The lowest number of divisions on record was in 1895, when only 168 took place in the two sessions. The following is a table showing the number of divisions taken part in by local members : Sir J. T.
